Chapter 4
Automata and Kleene’s Theorem

We will now provide an
operational
view of regular languages through automata and, more abstractly, coalgebra.
The tools discussed here will provide the groundwork for the completeness proof in
Chapter

5
.
This chapter is divided in three parts: first, we present the operational semantics of regular expressions; second, we dive into the coalgebraic view on automata and languages; third, we prove Kleene’s theorem, which states that the denotational and operational semantics of regular expressions coincide — i.e., they provide equivalent semantics.

4.1
Derivatives

We start by introducing the notion of
derivative
, which is central to the study of the operational semantics of regular expressions.
We will define two functions: one that assigns to each expression an
output value
in
\{0,1\}
signaling whether computation can terminate successfully; and another that given a letter
\mathtt{a}\in A
returns its
\mathtt{a}
-derivative
, which is an expression that denotes all the words obtained from the first expression after
deleting
an
\mathtt{a}
.
These functions were first proposed by Brzozowski
[
14
]
, which is why they are known as
Brzozowski derivatives
.

Definition 4.1

(Brzozowski derivatives
[
14
]
)
.

The
output function

o\colon\mathsf{Exp}\to 2
is defined inductively, as follows:

\displaystyle o(0)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle o(1)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle o(\mathtt{a})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle o(e+f)

\displaystyle=o(e)\vee o(f)

\displaystyle o(ef)

\displaystyle=o(e)\land o(f)

\displaystyle o(e^{*})

\displaystyle=1

Here,
b_{1}\vee b_{2}
is understood to be
0
when
b_{1}=b_{2}=0
, and
1
otherwise; similarly,
b_{1}\wedge b_{2}
is defined as
1
when
b_{1}=b_{2}=1
and
0
otherwise.

We furthermore define the
transition function

t\colon\mathsf{Exp}\to\mathsf{Exp}^{A}
inductively as follows, where we use
e_{\mathtt{a}}
as a shorthand for
t(e)(\mathtt{a})
:

0_{\mathtt{a}}=1_{\mathtt{a}}=0\and(e+f)_{\mathtt{a}}=e_{\mathtt{a}}+f_{\mathtt{a}}\and(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}=e_{\mathtt{a}}e^{*}\\
\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{a}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathtt{a}=\mathtt{b}\\
0&\text{if }\mathtt{a}\neq\mathtt{b}\end{cases}\and(ef)_{\mathtt{a}}=\begin{cases}e_{\mathtt{a}}f&\text{if }o(e)=0\\
e_{\mathtt{a}}f+f_{\mathtt{a}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

For a regular expression
e
, we have
o(e)=1
iff
\llbracket e\rrbracket
contains the empty word
\varepsilon
, while
e_{\mathtt{a}}
denotes the language containing all words
w
such that
\mathtt{a}w
is in the language denoted by
e
.
The derivative for sequential composition can alternatively be presented more compactly as
(ef)_{\mathtt{a}}=e_{\mathtt{a}}f+o(e)f_{\mathtt{a}}
, by observing that
o(e)\in 2\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Exp}
.

Example 4.2
.

Consider the regular expression
e=(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}
.
By definition,
o(e)=1
, meaning that
\llbracket e\rrbracket
should contain the empty word — which is indeed the case.
Moreover, we can calculate
e_{\mathtt{a}}
as follows:

\displaystyle e_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle=(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})_{\mathtt{a}}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}=((\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b})_{\mathtt{a}}+\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{a}})(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}

\displaystyle=(\mathtt{a}_{\mathtt{a}}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{a}})(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}=(1\cdot\mathtt{b}+0)(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\equiv\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}

In other words,
\mathtt{a}w\in\llbracket e\rrbracket
should hold if and only if
w=\mathtt{b}w^{\prime}
with
w^{\prime}\in\llbracket e\rrbracket
— and a cursory inspection of
e
tells us that this is true.

Thus, derivatives tell us how to “deconstruct” a regular expression in terms of its
immediate
behavior (whether or not the empty word is accepted), and its
later
behavior (the words denoted that begin with a given letter).
The next result, which is useful on a number of occasions, validates this intuition by telling us that this correspondence goes both ways: an expression can be “reconstructed” from its derivatives.

Theorem 4.3

(Fundamental theorem of Kleene Algebra
[
14
, Theorem 6.4]
)
.

Every regular expression
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
satisfies

e\equiv{o(e)}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}e_{\mathtt{a}}

Remark 4.4

(Generalized sum)
.

In the statement above, and in the sequel, we use a “generalized sum” operator
\sum
, interpreted intuitively the same as for numbers.
This is a slight abuse of notation, because we use it to define a
term
, and it is not clear whether
\sum\{e,f,g\}
denotes
f+(e+g)
or
e+(g+f)
, or any other term described as “the sum of
\{e,f,g\}
”.
However, associativity and commutativity of
+
up to
\equiv
means that order and bracketing of sums does not matter in most contexts.

In a similar vein, we will often implicitly rely on familiar properties of summation that are valid in Kleene Algebra as well (in addition to the idempotence law
e+e\equiv e
) for the sake of brevity.
Concretely, we will freely use the following properties of sums in the proof that follows, as well as later on, for all finite
X,Y\subseteq\mathsf{Exp}
and
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
:

e+\sum_{f\in X}f\equiv\sum_{f\in X\cup\{e\}}f\and\sum_{f\in X\cup Y}f\equiv\sum_{f\in X}f+\sum_{f\in Y}f\and e\Big(\sum_{f\in X}f\Big)\equiv\sum_{f\in X}e\cdot f\and\Big(\sum_{f\in X}f\Big)e\equiv\sum_{f\in X}f\cdot e

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

We proceed by induction on the structure of
e
.
The base cases are direct consequences of the laws for
0
and
1
.

•

When
e=0
, we derive as follows.

0\equiv 0+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}0\equiv o(0)+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}0_{\mathtt{a}}

•

Next, when
e=1
, we can calculate

1\equiv 1+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}0\equiv o(1)+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}1_{\mathtt{a}}

•

Finally, when
e=\mathtt{b}
for some
\mathtt{b}\in A
, we find that

\mathtt{b}\equiv 0+\mathtt{b}1+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A\setminus\{\mathtt{b}\}}\mathtt{a}0\equiv o(\mathtt{b})+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{a}}

This leaves us with three inductive cases.
For each, our induction hypothesis is that the statement is true for the proper subterms.

•

When
e=e_{1}+e_{2}
, we find that:

\displaystyle e_{1}+e_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\Big({o(e_{1})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}\Big)+\Big({o(e_{2})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}\Big)

(IH)

\displaystyle\equiv{o(e_{1})}+{o(e_{2})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}((e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}+(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}})

\displaystyle\equiv{o(e_{1}+e_{2})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1}+e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}

•

When
e=e_{1}e_{2}
, we derive:

\displaystyle e_{1}e_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\Big({o(e_{1})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}\Big)e_{2}

(IH)

\displaystyle\equiv{o(e_{1})}e_{2}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv{o(e_{1})}\Big({o(e_{2})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}\Big)+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2}

(IH)

\displaystyle\equiv{o(e_{1}e_{2})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}((e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2}+{o(e_{1})}(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}})

\displaystyle\equiv{o(e_{1}e_{2})}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1}e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}

Note how in this case the induction hypotheses for
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are applied subsequently, and not at the same time.

•

When
e=e_{1}^{*}
, the proof is a little bit more tricky.
In the following derivation, we write
x
as a shorthand for
\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}
.
We have:

\displaystyle e_{1}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{IH}}}{{\equiv}}({o(e_{1})}+x)^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dagger}}{{\equiv}}x^{*}\equiv 1+xx^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dagger}}{{\equiv}}1+x({o(e_{1})}+x)^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{IH}}}{{\equiv}}1+xe_{1}^{*}

where in the two steps marked with (
\dagger
), we use that for all
f\in\mathsf{Exp}
,
(f+1)^{*}\equiv f^{*}
(cf.
2.9
).
We can then finish the proof:

\displaystyle 1+xe_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle\equiv 1+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{1}^{*}

(distributivity)

\displaystyle\equiv o(e_{1}^{*})+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}(e_{1}^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}

∎

Remark 4.5
.

The name
Fundamental Theorem
is borrowed from
[
15
]
, where a similar property is proved for formal power series (functions
f\colon A^{*}\to K
, for a semiring
K
).
As explained there, the name is chosen in analogy to calculus: if we view prefixing with
\mathtt{a}
as a kind of integration, then the theorem tells us that derivation is the opposite of integration, by obtaining
e
from the
\mathtt{a}
-derivatives
e_{\mathtt{a}}
and the initial value
o(e)
.

We end this section with the semantic version of the Fundamental Theorem, which follows by soundness of the axioms.

Corollary 4.6
.

For all
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
, we have that
\llbracket e\rrbracket=\llbracket{o(e)}+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}e_{\mathtt{a}}\rrbracket
.

4.2
Automata (coalgebraically)

The semantics of regular expressions
\llbracket-\rrbracket
is
denotational
, in the sense that the interpretation of a regular expression arises from the that of its subexpressions.
However, there is another way to assign semantics to regular expressions, which arises from the
operational
perspective.
Here, we think of an expression as an
abstract machine
, which can evolve by performing certain computational steps; informally, the semantics of such an object is then taken as the set of all possible action sequences.

Remark 4.7
.

The definitions in this section will be presented in an elementary fashion.
However, readers who have gone through
Chapter

3
will be able to relate the definitions and claims here to the corresponding abstractions there, when instantiated with the appropriate functor.
The remainder of this section is presented without this abstraction; we have inserted remarks with a blue background (like this one) to make the connection explicit.
Readers who skipped
Chapter

3
can disregard these.

More specifically, the machines studied are the well-known
(deterministic) automata
; they form the operational model of regular expressions.

Definition 4.8
.

A
(deterministic) automaton
is a pair
(X,d_{X})
where
X
is a set, and
d_{X}\colon X\to 2\times X^{A}
is a function.
We will often write
d_{X}
as
<o_{X},t_{X}>
, where
o_{X}\colon X\to 2
and
t_{X}\colon X\to X^{A}
are functions such that
d_{X}(x)=<o_{X}(x),t_{X}(x)>
.
We say
(X,d_{X})
is finite when
X
is.

Remark 4.9
.

Clearly, an automaton as defined above is precisely a coalgebra for the functor
D
given by
D(X)=2\times X^{A}
(cf.
3.3
).

We often denote
t_{X}(x)(\mathtt{a})
with
(x)_{\mathtt{a}}
when
t_{X}
is clear from context.
Each
x\in X
is a
state
, and when
o_{X}(x)=1
we call
x

accepting
.
We can draw an automaton as a graph where states are nodes (with accepting states circled twice) and transitions are labelled edges.
For instance, we can depict the automaton
(X,<o_{X},t_{X}>)
where
X=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
, and
o_{X}
and
t_{X}
are as given below on the left with the graph below on the right.

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}o_{X}(x_{1})&=&0&o_{X}(x_{2})&=&1\\
(x_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}&=&x_{2}&(x_{1})_{\mathtt{b}}&=&x_{1}\\
(x_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}&=&x_{1}&(x_{2})_{\mathtt{b}}&=&x_{2}\end{array}\and\hbox to137.18pt{\vbox to55.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.02873pt\lower-13.20001pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Remark 4.10
.

The reader may be more familiar with automata presented as tuples
(Q,\delta,F)
, where
Q
is a set of
states
,
F\subseteq Q
holds the
accepting states
, and
\delta\colon Q\times A\to Q
is called the
transition function
.
1
1

1

Many texts also choose to include an
initial state
in the definition.
The automata defined here are agnostic in this respect, because many proofs become easier when formulated as a property of all states.
Additionally, the connection to coalgebra is clearer if we forget about the initial state.

We can convert between these presentations easily, by sending
(X,<o_{X},t_{X}>)
to
(X,\delta,F)
, where
\delta(x,\mathtt{a})=(x)_{\mathtt{a}}
, and
F=\{x\in X\mid o_{X}(x)=1\}
; conversely,
(Q,\delta,F)
can be turned into
(X,<o_{X},t_{X}>)
by choosing
o_{X}(x)=1
when
x\in F
and
o_{X}(x)=0
otherwise, while
(x)_{\mathtt{a}}=\delta(x,\mathtt{a})
.

The Brzozowski derivatives
o\colon\mathsf{Exp}\to 2
and
t\colon\mathsf{Exp}\to\mathsf{Exp}^{A}
equip
\mathsf{Exp}
with an automaton structure, making
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
the (infinite)
Brzozowski automaton
; we return to this in
Section

4.2.3
.

Another important example of an automaton is carried by the set of languages
2^{A^{*}}
.
For
\mathtt{a}\in A
,
l_{\mathtt{a}}
is defined by
l_{\mathtt{a}}(w)=l(\mathtt{a}w)
.
Whether or not
l
is accepting is given by
l(\varepsilon)
.
Together, these define an automaton
(2^{A^{*}},<o_{L},t_{L}>)
.
These maps are sometimes called
semantic Brzozowski derivatives
, or
simply language derivatives
.

4.2.1
Morphisms and finality

A central concept in the discussion to come is the idea of a
morphism
.
Intuitively, this is a map that respects input and output derivatives.

Definition 4.11
.

Given two deterministic automata
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
, a function
h\colon S\to T
is a
homomorphism
if it is compatible with the output and transition functions in the sense that
o_{T}(h(s))=o_{S}(s)
, and
t_{T}(h(s))(\mathtt{a})=h(t_{S}(s)(\mathtt{a}))
, for all
\mathtt{a}\in A
.

Remark 4.12
.

Morphisms between automata as defined above are precisely morphisms between
D
-coalgebras — that is, the equations above correspond to the commutativity of the diagram below.

S
T
2\times S^{A}
2\times T^{A}
<o_{S},t_{S}>
<o_{T},t_{T}>
h
D(h)

Given an automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
, the derivative
s_{\mathtt{a}}
of a state
s
for input
\mathtt{a}\in A
can be inductively extended to the word derivative
s_{w}
of
s
for input
w\in A^{*}
, by defining
s_{\varepsilon}=s
and
s_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}=(s_{\mathtt{a}})_{w^{\prime}}
.
This enables an easy definition of the semantics of a state
s
of a deterministic automaton: the language
L(s)\in 2^{A^{*}}
recognized by
s
is given by:

L(s)(w)=o_{S}(s_{w})

(4.1)

Example 4.13
.

The language recognized by state
s_{1}
of the automaton on page
4.2
is the set of all words with an odd number of
\mathtt{a}
’s.
It is easy to check that, for example,
L(s_{1})(\mathtt{bab})=o_{S}((s_{1})_{\mathtt{bab}})=o_{S}(s_{2})=1
.

L
is defined from the state space of any automaton to
2^{A^{*}}
.
This function takes a very special place as a particular morphism that relates every automaton to the automaton formed by language derivatives.

Theorem 4.14

(Finality)
.

For any automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
,
L\colon S\to 2^{A^{*}}
is the
only
homomorphism from
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
to
(2^{A^{*}},<o_{L},t_{L}>)
.

Remark 4.15
.

Thus
(2^{A^{*}},<o_{L},t_{L}>)
forms the final
D
-coalgebra.

Proof.

We have to prove that the diagram commutes and that
L
is unique.
First the commutativity with the derivatives:

\displaystyle o_{L}(L(s))

\displaystyle=L(s)(\varepsilon)=o_{S}(s_{\varepsilon})=o_{S}(s)

\displaystyle t_{L}(L(s))(\mathtt{a})(w)

\displaystyle=L(s)_{\mathtt{a}}(w)=L(s)(\mathtt{a}w)

\displaystyle=L(s_{\mathtt{a}})(w)=L(t_{S}(s))(\mathtt{a})(w)

For the second-to-last step, note that, by definition of
L
(see (
4.1
)):

L(s)(\mathtt{a}w)=o_{S}(s_{\mathtt{a}w})=o_{S}((s_{\mathtt{a}})_{w})=L(s_{\mathtt{a}})(w)

For the uniqueness, suppose there is a morphism
h\colon S\to 2^{A^{*}}
such that, for every
s\in S
and
\mathtt{a}\in A
,
o_{L}(h(s))=o_{S}(s)
and
h(s)_{\mathtt{a}}=h(s_{\mathtt{a}})
.

We prove by induction on
w\in A^{*}
that
h(s)(w)=L(s)(w)
:

\displaystyle h(s)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=o_{L}(h(s))=o_{S}(s)=L(s)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle h(s)(\mathtt{a}w)

\displaystyle=(h(s)_{\mathtt{a}})(w)=h(s_{\mathtt{a}})(w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mathrm{IH})}}{{=}}L(s_{\mathtt{a}})(w)=L(s)(\mathtt{a}w)

∎

On its own,
Theorem

4.14
may seem abstract.
Its most immediate consequence is that we can relate homomorphisms to the language semantics of automata, by exploiting uniqueness.

Corollary 4.16
.

Given automata
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
with a morphism
h\colon S\to T
, it holds for
s\in S
that
L(s)=L(h(s))
.

Proof.

Follows from
Theorem

4.14
and the fact that
L\colon S\to 2^{A^{*}}
and
L\circ h\colon S\to 2^{A^{*}}
are morphisms from
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
to
(2^{A^{*}},<o_{L},t_{L}>)
.
∎

4.2.2
Bisimulations and coinduction

A moment ago, we saw how states related by homomorphisms must have the same language.
Unfortunately, the converse is not necessarily true: there exist automata with states that have the same language, but which are not related by any homomorphism (see
4.2
).
To get more mileage, we need a slightly more general notion, as follows.

Definition 4.17

(Bisimulation
[
16
]
)
.

Given
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
, we say that a relation
R\subseteq S\times T
is a
bisimulation
if
<s,t>\in R
implies

o_{S}(s)=o_{T}(t)\ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ (s_{\mathtt{a}},t_{\mathtt{a}})\in R\text{ for all }\mathtt{a}\in A

We write
s\leftrightarroweq t
whenever there exists a bisimulation
R
containing
(s,t)
.

Remark 4.18
.

Unsurprisingly, bisimulations between automata as defined above are in one-to-one correspondence with bisimulations between
D
-coalgebras — the conditions above guarantee the existence of a
D
-coalgebra structure on
R
, and vice versa; see
3.12
.

The next theorem guarantees that bisimilarity is a sound and complete proof principle for language equivalence of automata.

Theorem 4.19

(Coinduction principle
[
16
]
)
.

Given two deterministic automata
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
with
s\in S
and
t\in T
:

s\leftrightarroweq t\iff L(s)=L(t)

Remark 4.20
.

Coalgebraically, the claim above follows immediately from
Theorem

4.14
, which tells us that
L
is the morphism into the final
D
-coalgebra, and the fact that
D
is kernel-compatible (
3.15
), which we can then use to invoke
Lemma

3.20
and
Corollary

3.27
.

Proof.

For the forward implication, let
R
be a bisimulation.
We prove, by induction on
w\in A^{*}
, that, for all
(s,t)\in R
,
w\in L(s)\Leftrightarrow w\in L(t)
.

\displaystyle L(s)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=o_{S}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\leftrightarroweq t}}{{=}}o_{T}(t)=L(t)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle L(s)(\mathtt{a}w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=o_{S}(s_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}})=o_{S}((s_{\mathtt{a}})_{w^{\prime}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(IH)}}}{{=}}o_{T}((t_{\mathtt{a}})_{w^{\prime}})=L(t)(\mathtt{a}w^{\prime})

The penultimate step follows by induction and the fact that
(s_{\mathtt{a}},t_{\mathtt{a}})\in R
.

The converse follows by the observation that the relation
\{(s,t)\mid L(s)=L(t)\}
is a bisimulation; we leave the details to
4.4
.
∎

The upshot of
Theorem

4.19
is that we can use coinduction to determine whether two states
s
and
t
of two deterministic automata
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
recognize the same language: it is enough to construct a bisimulation containing
(s,t)
— indeed, if such a bisimulation does not exist, then the states
cannot
have the same language.

Example 4.21

(Coinduction)
.

Let
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
be the deterministic automata over the alphabet
\{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\}
given by

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The relation
R=\{<s_{1},t_{1}>,<s_{2},t_{1}>,<s_{3},t_{2}>\}
is a
bisimulation:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad\qquad}l}o_{S}(s_{1})=o_{S}(s_{2})=0=o_{T}(t_{1})&o_{S}(s_{3})=1=o_{T}(t_{2})\\[4.30554pt]
(s_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}=s_{2}\mathrel{R}t_{1}=(t_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}&(s_{1})_{\mathtt{b}}=s_{3}\mathrel{R}t_{2}=(t_{1})_{\mathtt{b}}\\[4.30554pt]
(s_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}=s_{2}\mathrel{R}t_{1}=(t_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}&(s_{2})_{\mathtt{b}}=s_{3}\mathrel{R}t_{2}=(t_{1})_{\mathtt{b}}\\[4.30554pt]
(s_{3})_{\mathtt{a}}=s_{3}\mathrel{R}t_{2}=(t_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}&(s_{3})_{\mathtt{b}}=s_{3}\mathrel{R}t_{2}=(t_{2})_{\mathtt{b}}\end{array}

Thus, by
Theorem

4.19
,
L(s_{1})=L(t_{1})=L(s_{2})
and
L(s_{3})=L(t_{3})
.

But how do we find a bisimulation?
Fortunately,
Definition

4.17
guides us: a bisimulation relate only states that agree on their acceptance, and must be closed under
\mathtt{a}
-transitions on both sides, for all
\mathtt{a}\in A
.
Thus, it suffices to start with the states of interest, and add pairs reachable by simultaneous
\mathtt{a}
-transitions, as long as those pairs agree on acceptance.
The algorithm in
Figure

4.1
implements this idea.

Figure 4.1:
A simple algorithm for deciding bisimilarity.

Data:

An automaton
(X,<o_{X},t_{X}>)
and states
x_{1},x_{2}\in X

Result:

A bisimulation
R
such that
x_{1}\mathrel{R}x_{2}
, or nothing

Initialize
R
to the empty set, and
T
to
\{<x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}>\}
;

while

\textnormal{{$T$}}\neq\emptyset

do

Remove
<x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}>
from
T
;

if

<x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}>\not\in R

then

if

o(x_{1}^{\prime})\neq o(x_{2}^{\prime})

then

//
No bisimulation is possible

return
;

Add
<x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}>
to
R
;

foreach

\mathtt{a}\in A

do

Add
<(x_{1}^{\prime})_{\mathtt{a}},(x_{2}^{\prime})_{\mathtt{a}}>
to
T
;

//
R
should now be a bisimulation

return

R
;

Theorem 4.22

(
[
16
]
)
.

Suppose that the input automaton
(X,<o_{X},t_{X}>)
is finite.
Then the algorithm in
Figure

4.1
is correct, in that it constructs a bisimulation if and only if
x_{1}
is bisimilar to
x_{2}
.

Proof sketch.

For termination, note that in each iteration either
R
stays the same and
T
shrinks, or
R
grows.
Because
R\subseteq X^{2}
and
X
is finite, the latter means that
X^{2}\setminus R
shrinks, and so the loop eventually terminates.

For partial correctness, the main loop satisfies these invariants:

1.

If
R^{\prime}\subseteq X^{2}
,
x_{1}\mathrel{R^{\prime}}x_{2}
, and
R^{\prime}
is a bisimulation, then
R\cup T\subseteq R^{\prime}
.

2.

If
x_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{R}x_{2}^{\prime}
, then
o(x_{1}^{\prime})=o(x_{2}^{\prime})
and
<(x_{1}^{\prime})_{\mathtt{a}},(x_{2}^{\prime})_{\mathtt{a}}>\in R\cup T
.

If the algorithm decides negatively in the loop for the pair
\langle x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
, suppose towards a contradiction that
x_{1}
is bisimilar to
x_{2}
.
Then there exists a bisimulation
R^{\prime}
on
X
such that
x_{1}\mathrel{R^{\prime}}x_{1}
.
But by the invariant,
x_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{R^{\prime}}x_{2}^{\prime}
, despite
o(x_{1}^{\prime})\neq o(x_{2}^{\prime})
.
This is a contradiction; we must therefore conclude that
x_{1}
is indeed not bisimilar to
x_{2}
.

Conversely, if the algorithm decides positively, then
T=\emptyset
.
The second invariant then implies that
R
is a bisimulation.
Since
x_{1}\mathrel{R}x_{2}
by construction, we must conclude that
x_{1}
is bisimilar to
x_{2}
.
∎

Of course, this algorithm leaves some room for optimization.
We refer to
Chapter

7
for pointers to further reading on these topics.

4.2.3
Soundness of Brzozowski derivatives

Recall that
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
is an automaton and thus, by Theorem
4.14
, we have a unique map
L\colon\mathsf{Exp}\to 2^{A^{*}}
that acts as a morphism between
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
and
(2^{A^{*}},<o_{L},t_{L}>)
.
Hence,
L
provides an alternative
operational
semantics for regular expressions.
We now prove that, for any
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
, this semantics coincides with the one from
Definition

2.8
.

Theorem 4.23

(
[
14
]
)
.

For all
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
and
w\in A^{*}
,
\llbracket e\rrbracket=L(e)
.
That is,

w\in\llbracket e\rrbracket\Leftrightarrow w\in L(e)

Proof.

By
Theorem

4.14
, it suffices to show that
\llbracket-\rrbracket
acts like a morphism from
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
to
(2^{A^{*}},<o_{L},t_{L}>)
.
Concretely, this comes down to proving that for all
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
, it holds that
o_{L}(\llbracket e\rrbracket)=o(e)
, and furthermore that for
\mathtt{a}\in A
we have that
\llbracket e_{\mathtt{a}}\rrbracket=\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\mathtt{a}}
.

For the first equality, we derive using
Theorem

4.3
that:

o_{L}(\llbracket e\rrbracket)=o_{L}(\llbracket o(e)+\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}e_{\mathtt{a}}\rrbracket)=o_{L}(\llbracket o(e)\rrbracket)=o(e)

In the second-to-last step, we use that for
b\in\{0,1\}
, it holds that
o_{L}(\llbracket b\rrbracket)=b
.
For the second equality, we again use
Theorem

4.3
:

\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\mathtt{a}}=\llbracket o(e)+\sum_{\mathtt{b}\in A}\mathtt{b}e_{\mathtt{b}}\rrbracket_{\mathtt{a}}=\llbracket\mathtt{a}e_{\mathtt{a}}\rrbracket_{\mathtt{a}}=\llbracket e_{\mathtt{a}}\rrbracket\qed

Thus, the denotational semantics of regular expressions coincides with the operational semantics.
Furthermore, because bisimulations are sound and complete for deciding language equivalence of states in automata,
e
and
f
have the same language precisely when they are bisimilar as states of the Brzozowski automaton.
This
almost
means that we can mechanize equivalence checking of expressions (through the algorithm in
Figure

4.1
); the only wrinkle is that the Brzozowski automaton is not finite.
We will return to this matter shortly.

4.3
Kleene’s theorem

In this section, we present Kleene’s theorem, which states the equivalence
between the class of languages recognized by finite deterministic
automata and the one denoted by regular expressions.

Theorem 4.24

(Kleene’s Theorem
[
3
,
17
,
18
]
)
.

For any
l\in 2^{A^{*}}
,
l=\llbracket e\rrbracket
, for some regular expression
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
if and only if
l=L(s)
for a state
s\in S
of a finite deterministic automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
.

The proof of this theorem amounts to constructing an equivalent regular expression from a state of a deterministic automaton and, conversely, to constructing a deterministic automaton which has a state that is equivalent to a given regular expression.
We will detail these constructions in the next two sections.

4.3.1
Expressions to automata

Given a regular expression
e
, we want to construct a finite deterministic automaton with the same language.
We have shown that
\mathsf{Exp}
, the set of regular expressions over
A
, carries an automaton structure given by Brzozowski derivatives.
Hence, an obvious way of constructing an automaton with a state equivalent to
e
is to carve out all states reachable from
e
in the Brzozowski automaton
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
.
However, even for very simple expressions, this may still yield an infinite automaton.
Take for example
\mathtt{a}^{**}
; computing derivatives for
\mathtt{a}
yields:

(\mathtt{a}^{**})_{\mathtt{a}}=1\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{a}^{**}\and(1\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{a}^{**})_{\mathtt{a}}=(0\mathtt{a}^{*}+1\mathtt{a}^{*})\mathtt{a}^{**}+1\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{a}^{**}\and((0\mathtt{a}^{*}+1\mathtt{a}^{*})\mathtt{a}^{**}+1\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{a}^{**})_{\mathtt{a}}=(0\mathtt{a}^{*}+0\mathtt{a}^{*}+1\mathtt{a}^{*})\mathtt{a}^{**}+1\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{a}^{**}+(1\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{a}^{**})_{\mathtt{a}}

All derivatives above are equivalent, but they are syntactically different and therefore they will not be identified as denoting the same state.
Indeed, it is not too hard to see that every time we take a new
\mathtt{a}
-step from the last expression generated, we obtain an expression that is larger than (and therefore different from) the expressions we have seen before.
Thus
\mathtt{a}^{**}
can reach infinitely many states in
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
.

To achieve finiteness, it suffices to remove duplicate expressions in sums.
This can be done by leveraging the laws of associativity, commutativity and idempotence of
+
(ACI in brief)
[
14
,
7
]
.
In order to make this precise, we use these laws to define an equivalence.

Definition 4.25
.

We define
\cong
as the smallest congruence on
\mathsf{Exp}
(w.r.t.
+
,
\cdot
and
*
) satisfying the following laws, for all
e,f,g\in\mathsf{Exp}
:

e+(f+g)\cong(e+f)+g\and e+f\cong f+e\and e+e\cong e

The first thing to note about
\cong
is that, besides relating expressions that are clearly equivalent, is also a bisimulation on
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
.

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
e,f\in\mathsf{Exp}
such that
e\cong f
.
Then
o(e)=o(f)
, and moreover
e_{\mathtt{a}}\cong f_{\mathtt{a}}
for all
\mathtt{a}\in A
.

Proof.

It suffices to check the laws that generate
\cong
.
For instance, if
e\cong f
because
e=g_{1}+g_{2}
and
f=g_{2}+g_{1}
, then
o(e)=o(g_{1})\vee o(g_{2})=o(g_{2})\vee o(g_{1})
, and
e_{\mathtt{a}}=(g_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}+(g_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}\cong(g_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}+(g_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}=f_{\mathtt{a}}
.

The cases for the other rules, as well as the rules that come from the fact that
\cong
is a congruence on
\mathsf{Exp}
, follow a similar pattern.
∎

The following provides a characterization of the derivative
e_{w}
, for a non-empty word
w\in A^{*}
and
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
, in terms of
\cong
.

Proposition 4.27
.

Let
w\in A^{*}
be non-empty and
e,e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{Exp}
.
Then, the following hold for the derivatives of
(e_{1}+e_{2})
,
e_{1}e_{2}
and
e^{*}
:

\displaystyle(e_{1}+e_{2})_{w}

\displaystyle\cong(e_{1})_{w}+(e_{2})_{w}

\displaystyle(e_{1}e_{2})_{w}

\displaystyle\cong(e_{1})_{w}e_{2}+(e_{2})_{x_{1}}+\cdots+(e_{2})_{x_{m}}

\displaystyle(e^{*})_{w}

\displaystyle\cong e_{y_{1}}e^{*}+\cdots+e_{y_{n}}e^{*}

for some
m,n\geq 0
and
w_{i},y_{j}\in A^{*}
.

Proof.

By induction on the length of
w
.
For the base, where
w=\mathtt{a}
, the equalities hold by definition of derivatives.
For the inductive step, where
w=\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}
for non-empty
w^{\prime}\in A^{*}
, the first claim is easy to prove:

(e_{1}+e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phantom{(\mathrm{IH})}}}{{=}}((e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}+(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}})_{w^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mathrm{IH})}}{{\cong}}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}+(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}

For the second claim, we note that

(e_{1}e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}((e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2})_{w^{\prime}}&\text{if }o(e_{1})=0\\
((e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2})_{w^{\prime}}+(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Both cases now follow by induction.
If
o(e_{1})=0
, we have

((e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2})_{w^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mathrm{IH})}}{{\cong}}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}w_{1}}e_{2}+(e_{2})_{x_{1}^{\prime}}+\cdots+(e_{2})_{x_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}}

Otherwise, if
o(e_{1})=1
, then we derive

((e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2})_{w^{\prime}}+(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mathrm{IH})}}{{\cong}}(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}w_{1}}e_{2}+(e_{2})_{x_{1}^{\prime}}+\cdots+(e_{2})_{x_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}}+(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}

Clearly, either of these expressions fit the format of the claim.

Finally, for the third equality, we need to use the result we have just proved for sequential composition:

\displaystyle(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phantom{(\mathrm{IH})}}}{{=}}(e_{\mathtt{a}}e^{*})_{w^{\prime}}\cong e_{\mathtt{a}w^{\prime}}e^{*}+(e^{*})_{x_{1}}+\cdots+(e^{*})_{x_{m}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mathrm{IH})}}{{\cong}}e_{y_{1}}e^{*}+\cdots+e_{y_{n}}e^{*}

for appropriately chosen
n
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\in A^{*}
.
∎

Using
Proposition

4.27
, we can now prove the following.

Proposition 4.28

(
[
14
]
)
.

Let
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
.
There exists a finite set of expressions
\rho(e)
such that for
w\in A^{*}
,
e_{w}\cong e^{\prime}
for some
e^{\prime}\in\rho(e)
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of the expression.
The base cases are trivial: they have either one, two or three distinct word derivatives.

For the sum
e_{1}+e_{2}
, we can choose
\rho(e_{1}+e_{2})=\{e_{1}^{\prime}+e_{2}^{\prime}\mid e_{1}^{\prime}\in\rho(e_{1}),e_{2}^{\prime}\in\rho(e_{2})\}
.
The claim then follows by induction and
Proposition

4.27
.
The cases for
e_{1}\cdot e_{2}
and
e_{1}^{*}
follow similarly.
∎

This was the final insight needed to construct a finite automaton for any given expression
e
.
The results come together as follows.

Theorem 4.29

(
[
14
]
)
.

Given an expression
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
, we can construct an automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
with a state
s\in S
such that
L(s)=\llbracket e\rrbracket
.

Proof.

We choose
S=\{[e^{\prime}]_{\cong}\mid e^{\prime}\in\rho(e)\}
, with
s=[e]_{\cong}
, while
o_{S}([e^{\prime}])=o(e^{\prime})
and
t_{S}([e^{\prime}]_{\cong},\mathtt{a})=[e^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}]_{\cong}
.
Since
\rho(e)
is finite, so is
S
; furthermore, since there exists an
e^{\prime}\in\rho(e)
with
e^{\prime}\cong e_{\varepsilon}=e
, we know that
s\in S
.
Moreover,
o_{S}
and
t_{S}
are well-defined, by
Proposition

4.26
.

Now, consider the automaton
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
where
T=\{e_{w}\mid w\in A^{*}\}
, and
o_{T}(e^{\prime})=o(e^{\prime})
, while
t_{T}(e^{\prime},\mathtt{a})=e^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}
.
Clearly, the injection of
T
into
\mathsf{Exp}
is a homomorphism, and hence the language of
e\in T
is precisely
L(e)=\llbracket e\rrbracket
.
Lastly
L(e)=L(s)
, because the map that sends
e^{\prime}\in T
to
[e^{\prime}]_{\cong}\in S
is a homomorphism from
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
to
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
.
∎

We can compute
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
as described in the proof above, by starting from
e
and incrementally exploring the state space by computing derivatives; every time we find an expression
e^{\prime}
that is
\cong
-equivalent to an expression
e^{\prime\prime}
seen before, we can route the transition to
e^{\prime}
instead.
This procedure is known as
Brzozowski’s construction
.
The example below illustrates how this algorithm proceeds in detail.

Example 4.30
.

Let
A=\{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\}
and
e=(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}
.
We compute derivatives incrementally, marking with
\checkmark
the derivatives that have been encountered before, and numbering the new ones:

In the last example, we strictly followed the procedure described above.
The goal was to construct a finite automaton with a state equivalent to
e
and we did not worry about its size.
If we allow expressions to be simplified even further, the resulting automaton could be much smaller.
Formally, we can do this by defining
\simeq
as the smallest congruence that contains
\cong
but also satisfies that
0e\simeq 0
,
1e\simeq e
, and
e+0\simeq e
for all
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
.
It is not too hard to verify that the properties of
\cong
can be transferred to
\simeq
, namely that (1) it is a bisimulation and (2) only finitely expressions can be reached from any expression, up to
\simeq
(since
\simeq
contains
\cong
, the latter follows immediately from
Proposition

4.28
).

Example 4.31
.

With
\simeq
, the calculations from
Example

4.30
become:

e_{\mathtt{}}

=

(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}

1

e_{\mathtt{a}}

=

(1\mathtt{b}+0)(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+0\mathtt{a}

\simeq\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}

2

e_{\mathtt{b}}

=

(0\mathtt{b}+1)(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+1\mathtt{a}

\simeq(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{a}

3

e_{\mathtt{aa}}

=

0(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}

\simeq 0

4

e_{\mathtt{ab}}

=

1(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}

\simeq e_{\mathtt{}}

\checkmark

e_{\mathtt{ba}}

=

(1\mathtt{b}+0)(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+0\mathtt{a}+1

\simeq\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+1

5

e_{\mathtt{bb}}

=

(0\mathtt{b}+1)(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+1\mathtt{a}+0

\simeq e_{\mathtt{b}}

\checkmark

e_{\mathtt{aaa}}

=

0

\simeq e_{\mathtt{aa}}

\checkmark

e_{\mathtt{aab}}

=

0

\simeq e_{\mathtt{aa}}

\checkmark

e_{\mathtt{baa}}

=

0(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+0

\simeq e_{\mathtt{aa}}

\checkmark

e_{\mathtt{bab}}

=

1(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}+0

\simeq e_{\mathtt{}}

\checkmark

The resulting automaton would then have
5
states:

e
e_{\mathtt{a}}
e_{\mathtt{ba}}
e_{\mathtt{aa}}
e_{\mathtt{b}}
\mathtt{a}
\mathtt{b}
\mathtt{b}
\mathtt{a}
\mathtt{a}
\mathtt{b}
\mathtt{a}
\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}
\mathtt{b}

The automaton in the last example is in fact minimal.
This is not always the case; for that one would have to consider the complete set of axioms from
Definition

2.16
.
However, in many cases the automaton computed using just a subset of the axioms is minimal: an empirical study about this phenomenon appears in
[
19
]
.

Remark 4.32
.

There are, of course, many more ways to construct an equivalent automaton from a regular expression.
We focused on Brzozowski’s construction here because it works well with the coalgebraic perspective, but you may be familiar with the syntax-directed translation proposed by Thompson
[
17
]
.
Other constructions can be found in
[
20
,
21
,
22
,
18
]
; we refer to
[
23
]
for an excellent overview.

4.3.2
Automata to expressions

To present the construction of a regular expression from a
state of a finite deterministic automaton we build a system of equations on expressions and then solve it.
Let
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
be a finite deterministic automaton.
We associate with each
s\in S
a variable
x_{s}
and an equation

\displaystyle x_{s}\equiv\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}x_{s_{\mathtt{a}}}+{o_{S}(s)}

(4.2)

The sum is well defined because
A
is finite.
In this way, we build a system with
n
equations and
n
variables, where
n
is the size of
S
.

Definition 4.33

(Solutions to systems of equations)
.

Consider a system of equations
x_{s}\equiv\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}x_{s_{\mathtt{a}}}+{o_{S}(s)},s\in S
as in

Equation

4.2
.
We define a
solution
to this system as a mapping that assigns to each variable
x_{s}
an expression
e_{s}\in\mathsf{Exp}
(over
A
) satisfying
e_{s}\equiv\sum_{\mathtt{a}\in A}\mathtt{a}e_{s_{\mathtt{a}}}+{o_{S}(s)}
.

Observe that if
e_{s}
is a solution to
x_{s}\equiv t_{s}
and if
t_{s}\equiv t_{s}^{\prime}
, then
e_{s}
is also a solution to
x_{s}\equiv t_{s}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, if the equation is of the shape
x\equiv tx+c
, then
t^{*}c
is a solution to the equation
[
6
]
.
This can be used to iteratively solve a system, see below for an example.

Our definition of solution is sound, in the sense that every solution is necessarily language equivalent to the corresponding state.
This can be shown directly (see
4.9
), but a rather elegant proof by coinduction (cf.
Section

4.2
and
4.10
) is also possible.

We will now illustrate the construction of solutions with an example, and then we
will present an alternative definition of the solution of a system of
equations of the same form as (
4.2
) using matrices.

Example 4.34
.

Consider the following deterministic automaton with two states, over the alphabet
A=\{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\}
:

s_{1}
s_{2}
\mathtt{a}
\mathtt{b}
\mathtt{b}
\mathtt{b}

Let
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
denote
e_{s_{1}}
and
e_{s_{2}}
, respectively.
The system of equations arising from the automaton is as follows:

e_{1}\equiv\mathtt{a}e_{2}+\mathtt{b}e_{1}+0\and e_{2}\equiv\mathtt{a}e_{1}+\mathtt{b}e_{2}+1

To solve for
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, we first apply the left fixpoint rule to the second equation to find that
\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)\leqq e_{2}
.
Indeed, if we fill in
\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)
for
e_{2}
in the second equation, it is not too hard to see that this constraint is still satisfied, regardless of the expression we end up choosing for
e_{1}
:

\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)\equiv(1+\mathtt{b}\mathtt{b}^{*})(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)\equiv\mathtt{a}e_{1}+\mathtt{b}\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)+1

Thus, if we find a closed expression for
e_{1}
satisfying the first equation, we can plug this into the above to find a closed expression for
e_{2}
such that the second equation holds.
Now, given that we expressed
e_{2}
in terms of
e_{1}
, we can replace
e_{2}
with
\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)
in the first equation:

e_{1}\geqq\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)+\mathtt{b}e_{1}+0\equiv(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})e_{1}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}

By the left fixpoint rule, this tells us that
(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\leqq e_{1}
.
If we take the left-hand side of this as our closed expression for
e_{1}
, we can plug it into
\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}e_{1}+1)
to find the following closed expression for
e_{2}
:

e_{2}\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}+1)\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}+\mathtt{b}^{*}

Together, these choices for
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
satisfy the equation for
e_{1}
; our previous arguments show that they should also satisfy the equation for
e_{2}
.
Lastly, and crucially, one can show that
\llbracket e_{1}\rrbracket=L(s_{1})
and
\llbracket e_{2}\rrbracket=L(s_{2})
.

The methodology above generalizes to larger automata, but can become tedious to work out by hand (we will return to it in
Chapter

5
).
For now, we briefly present an alternative general method
[
24
]
to construct a solution to a system of
n
equations of the form

x_{i}\equiv e_{i1}x_{1}+\cdots+e_{in}x_{n}+f_{i}\ \ \ \ \ 1\leq i\leq n

Given a system of
n
equations as above, construct an
n\times n
matrix
M
with all the
e_{ij}
, an
n
-dimensional vector
O
with all the
f_{i}
, and an
n
-dimensional vector
X
with all the
x_{i}
.
The constraints of the above form can then collectively be written as the matrix-vector equation below, where matrix-vector multiplication and vector addition works just like in linear algebra, and vector equivalence is interpreted pointwise:

X\equiv MX+O

(4.3)

Next, we define, for an
n\times n
matrix
R
with entries in
\mathsf{Exp}
, a matrix
R^{*}
, by induction on
n
.
For
n=1
, the matrix
R
has a single entry
e
and
R^{*}=\left[e^{*}\right]
.
If
n\mathchar 8510\relax 1
, we divide the matrix into four submatrices

R=\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&V\end{pmatrix}

where
S
and
V
are square with dimensions, respectively,
m\times m
(for some
0\mathchar 8508\relax m\mathchar 8508\relax n
) and
(n-m)\times(n-m)
.
By the induction hypothesis, we can compute the “star” of square matrices strictly smaller than
R
; this includes the submatrices
S
and
V
, but also other
m
-by-
m
matrices.
This allows us to define
R^{*}
as follows:

R^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}(S+TV^{*}U)^{*}&(S+TV^{*}U)^{*}TV^{*}\\
(V+US^{*}T)^{*}US^{*}&(V+US^{*}T)^{*}\end{pmatrix}

Remark 4.35
.

The choice of expressions in
R^{*}
may seem magical at first, but can be explained intuitively by thinking of
R
as encoding the transitions of a two-state automaton, with
S
the label of a transition from the first state to itself,
T
as the label of the transition from the first to the second state, and so on.
Then,
R^{*}
contains expressions for the words read along paths between the state — e.g.,
(S+TV^{*}U)^{*}
describes the paths from the first state to itself, which consist of either self-transitions (
S
), or transitions to the second state (
T
), a number of loops there (
V^{*}
), and a transition back (
U
).
See also
Example

4.36
.

Now we can compute
M^{*}
, and show that the vector
M^{*}O
is the least (with respect to the
pointwise order
\leqq
of regular expressions) solution to the system
in (
4.3
)
[
25
, Theorem A.3]
.

Example 4.36
.

Let us revisit
Example

4.34
using this method.
First, we construct the matrices
X
,
M
and
O
:

X=\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \ M=\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{b}&\mathtt{a}\\
\mathtt{a}&\mathtt{b}\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \ O=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \ \

The two equations are now captured by
MX+O\equiv X
.
We can now solve for
X=M^{*}O
by computing
M^{*}
, as follows:

M^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}&(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\\
(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}&(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\end{pmatrix}\\
M^{*}O=\begin{pmatrix}((\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*})0+((\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*})1\\
((\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*})0+((\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*})1\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\\
(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\end{pmatrix}

In the last step we used some of the Kleene algebra equations to simplify regular expressions.
Now note that the expression computed for
e_{1}
is almost the same as the one we obtained in
Example

4.34
, whereas the one for
e_{2}
is quite different (syntactically), but still equivalent.

With the constructions from deterministic automata to regular expressions, and vice versa, proving Kleene’s theorem is straightforward.

Proof of Theorem
4.24
(Kleene’s Theorem).

Suppose
l=L(e)
for some regular expression
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
.
We can construct an automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and accompanying state that accepts the same language as
e
, as described in
Theorem

4.29
.
For the converse, suppose
l=L(s)
, for state
s\in S
of a finite deterministic automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
.
We solve a system of equations as described in
Definition

4.33
and the result is an expression
e_{s}
equivalent to
s
, that is
\llbracket e_{s}\rrbracket=L(s)
.
∎

To summarize, in this chapter we instantiated the coalgebraic framework to deterministic automata, obtaining a decidable notion of
bisimulation
that characterizes language equivalence (
Section

4.2
).
We then showed that every regular expression can be converted into an equivalent deterministic automaton, meaning equivalence of regular expressions can be checked (
Section

4.3.1
).
Finally, we argued that every automaton can be converted back to an expression (
Section

4.3.2
).

Exercises

4.1.

of words where every third letter is an
\mathtt{a}
, unless it is preceded by a

.

include

’s.
Words in

4.5.

In the proof of

, we claimed that the following two properties were

— that is, if they are true before the loop body starts, then they are also true when the loop body ends:

1.

If

.

.

Complete the following tasks regarding these claims:

(a)

Prove that the first property is truly an invariant.

(b)

Prove that the second property is truly an invariant.

(c)

),

is a bisimulation relating
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
.

(d)

Prove that, when the loop ends successfully,

is the

and

— that is, if

is any bisimulation relating

.

.

Show that

is a bisimulation between

and the Brzozowski coalgebra, and conclude that

for all
