.

Chapter 5
Completeness

In the previous chapters, we presented regular expressions, their denotational and operational semantics, and a set of laws to reason about their equivalence.
In this chapter, we want to prove that the laws of Kleene Algebra are enough to reason about any equivalence of regular expressions.
That is, given two regular expressions
e
and
f
that denote the same regular language, we can prove that
e\equiv f
.
This property is called
completeness
with respect to the semantic model.
The goal of this chapter is therefore to show that the laws of Kleene Algebra are
complete
for the language model.
We will use the fact that language equivalence and bisimilarity coincide for regular expressions, and show:

Theorem 5.1

(Soundness and completeness)
.

Let
e,f\in\mathsf{Exp}
; now

e\equiv f\iff\text{$e\leftrightarroweq f$ in $(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)$}

The forward implication, called
soundness
, says that all equivalences proved using the laws of
\mathsf{KA}
are in fact between bisimilar expressions.
The converse, called
completeness
, says that all true equations between regular expressions can be proved using only the laws of
\mathsf{KA}
.

Many of the results presented in this chapter have appeared in the literature (e.g.,
[
7
,
8
,
26
]
).
We will include proofs inspired by these works, and in some cases we will present them differently.
In particular, we give a new account using
locally finite matrices
which fills in a gap in previous coalgebraic proofs.

5.1
Matrices over Kleene algebras

We will start with what is perhaps the single most useful observation about Kleene algebra, which comes down to the following slogan:

Matrices over a Kleene algebra form a Kleene algebra!

We will first develop this idea for finite matrices.
Matrices indexed by infinite sets are a bit more subtle, and we will spend some time working on that in the latter half of this section.
For the remainder of this section, we fix a Kleene algebra
\mathcal{K}=(X,+,\cdot,-^{*},0,1)
.

5.1.1
Finite matrices

To get an idea of how our slogan works for finite matrices, consider
2\times 2
-matrices formed using elements of a Kleene algebra
\mathcal{K}
.
We can define addition and multiplication of these matrices in the usual way:

\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e_{4}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}e_{5}&e_{6}\\
e_{7}&e_{8}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}+e_{5}&e_{2}+e_{6}\\
e_{3}+e_{7}&e_{4}+e_{8}\end{pmatrix},\\
\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e_{4}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}e_{5}&e_{6}\\
e_{7}&e_{8}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}e_{5}+e_{2}e_{7}&e_{1}e_{6}+e_{2}e_{8}\\
e_{3}e_{5}+e_{4}e_{7}&e_{3}e_{6}+e_{4}e_{8}\end{pmatrix}.

It should be clear that the neutral elements for
+
and
\cdot
are, respectively:

\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\and\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

The “only” question that remains is: how do we define an operation on matrices that satisfies the axioms of the Kleene star?
As it turns out, for
n=2
, the following is a valid choice:

\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e_{4}\end{pmatrix}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}(e_{1}+e_{2}e_{4}^{*}e_{3})^{*}&(e_{1}+e_{2}e_{4}^{*}e_{3})^{*}e_{2}e_{4}^{*}\\
(e_{4}+e_{3}e_{1}^{*}e_{2})^{*}e_{3}e_{1}^{*}&(e_{4}+e_{3}e_{1}^{*}e_{2})^{*}\end{pmatrix},

This may look like an arbitrary soup of symbols right now, although the eagle-eyed reader might recognize the operation on matrices from page
4.3.2
, defined to solve systems of equations.
We will derive a way to compute the star of
n\times n
-matrices for a given
n
in a moment; for now, let us pause and formalize what we already know.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
Q
be a set.
A
Q
-vector (over
\mathcal{K}
)
is a function
v\colon Q\to\mathcal{K}
, and a
Q
-matrix (over
\mathcal{K}
)
is a function
M\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
.

Addition of matrices and vectors is defined pointwise, e.g., for
Q
-matrices
M
and
N
, we define
M+N
as the
Q
-matrix defined by:

(M+N)(q,q^{\prime})=M(q,q^{\prime})+N(q,q^{\prime})

When
Q
is finite, matrix-matrix and matrix-vector multiplication works as usual: for
Q
-matrices
M
and
N
and
Q
-vectors
v
, we have that
M\cdot N
and
M\cdot v
are respectively the
Q
-matrix and
Q
-vector defined by:

(M\cdot N)(q,q^{\prime})=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot N(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})\and(M\cdot v)(q)=\sum_{q^{\prime}}M(q,q^{\prime})\cdot v(q^{\prime})

Note how the sums on the right-hand side are finite, because
Q
is finite.

Finally, given a
Q
-vector
v
and
x\in\mathcal{K}
, we write
v\fatsemi x
for the
(right) scalar multiplication
of
v
by
x
, which is simply the
Q
-vector where

(v\fatsemi x)(q)=v(q)\cdot x

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
X
,
Y
and
Z
be finite
Q
-matrices.
We write
\mathbf{1}
for the identity
Q
-matrix, given by
\mathbf{1}(q,q)=[q=q^{\prime}]
, where we use
[q=q^{\prime}]
as a shorthand for
1
when
q=q^{\prime}
, and
0
otherwise.
1
1

1

We will use this
Iverson bracket
in the sequel, too.

Furthermore, we write
\mathbf{0}
for the
Q
-matrix where
\mathbf{0}(q,q^{\prime})=0
.
The following hold:

X+\mathbf{0}=X\and X+X=X\and X+Y=Y+X\\
X+(Y+Z)=(X+Y)+Z\and X\cdot(Y\cdot Z)=(X\cdot Y)\cdot Z\\
X\cdot(Y+Z)=X\cdot Y+X\cdot Z\and(X+Y)\cdot Z=X\cdot Z+Y\cdot Z\\
X\cdot\mathbf{1}=X=X\cdot\mathbf{1}\and X\cdot\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{0}\cdot X

Proof.

Each law can be proved by unfolding the definitions of the operators, and applying the corresponding law on the level of
\mathcal{K}
.

5.1
highlights the left distributivity law.
∎

Because the
+
operation on matrices satisfies the same laws as expected from the
+
in a Kleene algebra, we automatically get an order defined on
Q
-matrices in the same way:
M\leq N
when
M+N=N
.

Next we show that finite matrices over a Kleene algebra satisfy the star axioms.
We gave a direct definition of the star of a matrix on page
4.3.2
.
For our purposes it is more practical to define the star of a matrix using least solutions.
These two definitions can be shown to be equivalent
[
25
, Theorem A.3]
.
We start with a technical construction.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
Q
be a finite set, with
M
a
Q
-matrix and
b
a
Q
-vector.
We can construct a
Q
-vector
s
such that
b+M\cdot s\leq s
, and furthermore if
t
is a
Q
-vector and
z\in\mathcal{K}
with
b\fatsemi z+M\cdot t\leq t
, then
s\fatsemi z\leq t
.

Proof.

Let us first look at the special case where
Q
is a singleton set.
Here,
M
and
b
contain just one element of
\mathcal{K}
— so we treat them as such.
Moreover, addition and multiplication comes down to adding and multiplying single elements.
Thus, we are really looking for
s\in\mathcal{K}
where
b+M\cdot s\leq s
, and if
t\in\mathcal{K}
such that
b\cdot z+M\cdot t\leq t
, then
s\cdot z\leq t
.
But we already know exactly such an expression:
M^{*}\cdot b
.
After all,

b+M\cdot M^{*}\cdot b=(1+M\cdot M^{*})\cdot b=M^{*}\cdot b\and b\cdot z+M\cdot t\leq t\implies M^{*}\cdot b\cdot z\leq t

Our job is to generalize this approach to sets
Q
that contain
more
elements.
To this end, we proceed by induction on (the size of)
Q
.

In the base, where
Q=\emptyset
, we can simply choose
s
to be the unique
Q
-vector
s\colon\emptyset\to\mathcal{K}
, which satisfies both conditions vacuously.
For the inductive step, let
p\in Q
and choose
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\{p\}
.
We are going to create a
Q^{\prime}
-vector and
Q^{\prime}
-matrix, and then use the induction hypothesis to obtain a
Q^{\prime}
-vector; next, we will extend this
Q^{\prime}
-vector into a
Q
-vector satisfying our objectives.

Let
M^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
respectively be the
Q^{\prime}
-matrix and
Q^{\prime}
-vector given by

M^{\prime}(q,q^{\prime})=M(q,q^{\prime})+M(q,p)\cdot{M(p,p)}^{*}\cdot M(p,q^{\prime})\and b^{\prime}(q)=b(q)+M(q,p)\cdot{M(p,p)}^{*}\cdot b(p)

The intuition behind these choices is as follows.
Let’s think of
M
and
b
as representing a generalized “automaton”, where
M(q,q^{\prime})
holds regular expressions for the words that can be read while moving from
q
to
q^{\prime}
in one “step”, and
b(q)
holds regular expressions for the words that can be read upon halting in
q
.
From this perspective,
M^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
represent an automaton meant to read the same words, just with
p
removed.
To achieve this,
M^{\prime}(q,q^{\prime})
holds all of the words that could already be read when stepping from
q
to
q^{\prime}
—
M(q,q^{\prime})
— plus the words that could be read by moving from
q
to
p
, looping at
p
some number of times, and then moving to
q^{\prime}
—
M(q,p)\cdot M(p,p)^{*}\cdot M(p,q^{\prime})
.
Similarly,
b^{\prime}(q)
is a regular expression for the words that could already be accepted at
q
—
b(q)
— in addition to the words that could be accepted by transitioning to
p
, looping there for a while, and ultimately accepting —
M(q,p)\cdot M(p,p)^{*}\cdot b(p)
.
Through this lens, the construction mirrors precisely the classical “state elimination” method to convert a finite automaton to a regular expression
[
18
]
.

By induction we obtain a
Q^{\prime}
-vector
s^{\prime}
such that
b^{\prime}+M^{\prime}\cdot s^{\prime}\leq s^{\prime}
, and moreover if
t^{\prime}
is a
Q^{\prime}
-vector such that
b^{\prime}\fatsemi z+M^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}\leq t^{\prime}
, then
s^{\prime}\fatsemi z\leq t^{\prime}
.

We extend
s
to a
Q
-vector as follows:

s(q)=\begin{cases}s^{\prime}(q)&q\in Q^{\prime}\\
{M(p,p)}^{*}\cdot\left(b(p)+\sum_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}M(p,q^{\prime})\cdot s^{\prime}(q^{\prime})\right)&q=p\end{cases}

We leave the proof that
s
satisfies our goal as
5.4
.
∎

This construction then immediately gives us a way to define the star of a matrix, which we record in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5

(Star of a matrix)
.

Let
Q
be a finite set, and let
M
be a
Q
-matrix.
We can construct a matrix
M^{*}
such that the following hold:

(i)

if
s
and
b
are
Q
-vectors s.t.
b+M\cdot s\leq s
, then
M^{*}\cdot b\leq s
; and

(ii)

\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*}=M^{*}
, where
\mathbf{1}
is as in
Lemma

5.3
.

Proof.

For each
q\in Q
, we write
u_{q}
for the
Q
-vector where
u_{q}(q^{\prime})=[q=q^{\prime}]
.
Furthermore, we write
s_{q}
for the
Q
-vector such that
u_{q}+M\cdot s_{q}\leq s_{q}
, and if
z\in\mathcal{K}
and
t
is a
Q
-vector such that
u_{q}\fatsemi z+M\cdot t\leq t
, then
s_{q}\fatsemi z\leq t
; note that we can construct this
Q
-vector, per
Lemma

5.4
.

We now choose the
Q
-matrix
M^{*}
as follows:

M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=s_{q^{\prime}}(q)

It remains to show that
M^{*}
satisfies the two requirements above.

(i)

Suppose that
s
and
b
are
Q
-vectors such that
b+M\cdot s\leq s
.
We need to show that
M^{*}\cdot b\leq s
, that is show that for all
q\in Q
:

\sum_{q^{\prime}\in Q}M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})\cdot b(q^{\prime})\leq s(q)

It suffices to show that, for all
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
, we have
s_{q^{\prime}}(q)\cdot b(q^{\prime})\leqq s(q)
, that is
s_{q^{\prime}}\fatsemi b(q^{\prime})\leq s
.
By construction of
s_{q^{\prime}}
, it then suffices to show that
u_{q^{\prime}}\fatsemi b(q^{\prime})+M\cdot s\leq s
.
To this end, we derive:

(u_{q^{\prime}}\fatsemi b(q^{\prime})+M\cdot s)(q)\leq(b+M\cdot s)(q)\leq s(q)

which completes this part of the proof.

(ii)

For the second claim, we first note that for
q\in Q
, we have

u_{q}+M\cdot(u_{q}+M\cdot s_{q})\leq u_{q}+M\cdot s_{q}

and hence
s_{q}\leq u_{q}+M\cdot s_{q}
, meaning that
u_{q}+M\cdot s_{q}=s_{q}
.

Now, let
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
; we derive as follows:

\displaystyle\phantom{=}(\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}(q,q^{\prime})+\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

(by def.)

\displaystyle=[q=q^{\prime}]+\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot s_{q^{\prime}}(q^{\prime\prime})

(def.
u_{q^{\prime}},M^{*}
)

\displaystyle=(u_{q^{\prime}}+M\cdot s_{q^{\prime}})(q)

(by def.)

\displaystyle=s_{q^{\prime}}(q)

(derivation above)

\displaystyle=M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})

(def.
M^{*}
) ∎

The left fixpoint rule now follows (cf.
5.5
).

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
Q
be a finite set, and let
M
and
B
be
Q
-matrices.
If
S
is a
Q
-matrix such that
B+M\cdot S\leq S
, then
M^{*}\cdot B\leq S
.

A proof of the remaining axioms — i.e., that (1)
\mathbf{1}+M^{*}\cdot M=M^{*}
and (2) if
B+S\cdot M\leq S
then
B\cdot M^{*}\leq S
— requires some thought.
We leave these to the reader in
5.6
, and record the following:

Theorem 5.7
.

The addition, multiplication and star operations on matrices satisfy all of the laws of Kleene algebra.
In particular, in addition to the laws listed in
Lemma

5.3
, if
X
,
Y
and
Z
are
Q
-matrices for some finite set
Q
, then

\mathbf{1}+X\cdot X^{*}=X^{*}\and X+Y\cdot Z\leq Z\implies Y^{*}\cdot Z\leq Z\\
\mathbf{1}+X^{*}\cdot X=X^{*}\and X+Y\cdot Z\leq Y\implies X\cdot Z^{*}\leq Y

5.1.2
Infinite matrices

In the proof of completeness to come, we will model finite automata using finite matrices, and their corresponding expressions using the Kleene star of those matrices.
However, we will also need to talk about potentially infinite automata.
It is not at all clear that the operations we defined on finite matrices still make sense for infinite ones, and if they do, whether the same laws apply.
In this subsection, we will study infinite matrices over a Kleene algebra, and argue that the same equations hold when the operations can be extended to infinite matrices.

The first operator to look at is addition.
Fortunately, there is no problem here at all: because addition of matrices is defined pointwise, it can be extended immediately to infinite matrices.
Furthermore, the laws that involve only addition, such as associativity, still work for the same reason that they do for finite matrices.

Next, we should look at multiplication.
Here, we encounter a problem when we try to extend the old definition.
Specifically, let
Q
be infinite, and let
M,N\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
.
If we try to define
M\cdot N
as usual, i.e.:

(M\cdot N)(q,q^{\prime})=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot N(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

we risk the possibility that the sum on the right is infinite!
This would be ill-defined, because we only have finitary addition in
\mathcal{K}
.
To work around this problem, we introduce the following notion.

Definition 5.8

(Locally-finite matrix)
.

Let
M\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
be a matrix for a possibly infinite set
Q
.
When
q\in Q
, we write
R_{M}(q)
for the
reach
of
q
, which is the smallest set satisfying the following rules:

\frac{~}{q\in R_{M}(q)}\and\frac{q^{\prime}\in R_{M}(q)\quad M(q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime})\neq 0}{q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)}

That is, if we regard
M
as the adjacency matrix of a directed graph, then
R_{M}(q)
is the set of vertices that can be reached from the vertex
q
.

We say that
M
is
locally-finite
when for each
q\in Q
,
R_{M}(q)
is finite.

Example 5.9
.

Every finite matrix is locally finite.
Examples of infinite but locally-finite matrices include the units
\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
; after all,
R_{\mathbf{0}}(q)=\emptyset
for all
q
, and
R_{\mathbf{1}}(q)=\{q\}
for all
q
.

Example 5.10
.

Consider the matrix
M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}
over
\equiv
-equivalence classes of expressions given by
M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}([e],[f])=\sum\{\mathtt{a}\mid e_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv f\}
.
This matrix is locally finite by
Proposition

4.28
and the observation that
R_{M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}}([e])=\{[e_{w}]\mid w\in A^{*}\}
; it will play a role in the completeness proof to follow.

Matrix multiplication makes sense when the left operand is locally finite.
Specifically, if
M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot N(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})\neq 0
, then certainly
M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\neq 0
, and if this is the case then
q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)
; because there are only finitely many such
q^{\prime\prime}
when
M
is locally-finite, there are only finitely many non-zero terms that contribute to the sum on the right.

Because matrix multiplication extends to some infinite matrices with the same definition, the same laws apply when both sides of the equation are defined.
For instance, if
X,Y,Z\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
are matrices such that
X
,
Y
and
X\cdot Y
are locally finite, then
X\cdot(Y\cdot Z)=(X\cdot Y)\cdot Z
.

Remark 5.11
.

As can be seen from the above, some care is necessary when stating equations involving operations on infinite matrices.
For one thing, it is not sufficient to assume that
X
and
Y
are locally finite, because
X\cdot Y
may not be.
Take for instance
Q=\mathbb{N}
and set

X(2k,2k+1)=1\and Y(2k+1,2k+2)=1

with all other entries of
X
and
Y
being zero.
In that case,
X
and
Y
are locally finite but
X\cdot Y
is not (nor is
X+Y
, for that matter).

For the sake of brevity, we will omit such qualifications from equalities involving infinite matrices going forward, although we will remark on when certain expressions are defined, just to be rigorous.

For the Kleene star, we also limit ourselves to locally finite matrices.
Intuitively, this makes sense because the Kleene star of a matrix corresponding to an automaton can be used to calculate a regular expression for the language of each state in that automaton.
If the Kleene star of a matrix could be extended to infinite matrices in general, it would mean that we could calculate regular expressions for
all
infinite automata — which would be problematic.
On the other hand, locally finite matrices correspond to (potentially infinite) automata in which every state can reach only finitely many states; it should be clear that in that setting, it is still possible to compute a regular expression for every state.

Definition 5.12
.

Let
M\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
be a matrix, with
Q
potentially infinite.
When
P\subseteq Q
, we write
M_{P}
for the
P
-indexed submatrix of
M
, i.e.,
M_{P}\colon P\times P\to\mathcal{K}
with
M_{P}(q,q^{\prime})=M(q,q^{\prime})
for
q,q^{\prime}\in P
.
This notation also extends to vectors, i.e., given a
Q
-vector
b\colon Q\to\mathcal{K}
, we write
b_{P}
for the
P
-vector given by
b_{P}(q)=b(q)
.

If
M
is locally finite, we write
M^{*}
for the
Q
-indexed matrix where

M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=\begin{cases}{(M_{R_{M}(q)})}^{*}(q,q^{\prime})&q^{\prime}\in R_{M}(q)\\
0&q^{\prime}\not\in R_{M}(q)\end{cases}

Note that when
M
is locally finite,
R_{M}(q)
is finite for every
q\in Q
, which means that
M_{R_{M}(q)}
is a finite matrix, hence
{(M_{R_{M}(q)})}^{*}
is defined.

It remains to verify the laws involving the Kleene star.
Let us start with
\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*}=M^{*}
.
We will need the following technical property, which says that, under certain conditions on
P
, its the star of its
P
-indexed submatrix is the
P
-indexed submatrix of its star.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
M\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
be a finite matrix, and let
P\subseteq Q
such that if
q\in P
and
M(q,q^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
q^{\prime}\in P
.
Now
(M_{P})^{*}=(M^{*})_{P}
.

Proof.

To see that
(M_{P})^{*}\leq(M^{*})_{P}
, unfolding the star on the left, it suffices to show that
M_{P}\cdot(M^{*})_{P}+\mathbf{1}\leq(M^{*})_{P}
.
To this end, we derive as follows, for
q,q^{\prime}\in P
:

\displaystyle(M_{P}\cdot(M^{*})_{P}+\mathbf{1})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in P}M_{P}(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot(M^{*})_{P}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})+[q=q^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in P}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})+[q=q^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})+[q=q^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=(M\cdot M^{*}+\mathbf{1})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=(M^{*})_{P}(q,q^{\prime})

To show that
(M^{*})_{P}\leq(M_{P})^{*}
, we need a slightly more involved argument.
Let
N\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
be the matrix defined by

N(q,q^{\prime})=\begin{cases}(M_{P})^{*}(q,q^{\prime})&q,q^{\prime}\in P\\
M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

It now suffices to show that
M^{*}\leq N
, because
N_{P}=(M_{P})^{*}
by construction.
To this end, we need only show that
M\cdot N+\mathbf{1}\leq N
.
There are two cases; first, if
q,q^{\prime}\in P
, then we derive

\displaystyle(M\cdot N+\mathbf{1})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot N(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})+[q=q^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in P}M_{P}(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot(M_{P})^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})+[q=q^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=(M_{P}\cdot(M_{P})^{*}+\mathbf{1})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(M_{P})^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=N(q,q^{\prime})

Here, the second step is sound because if
q\in P
and
M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\neq 0
, then
q^{\prime\prime}\in P
as well.
For the other case, where
q\not\in P
or
q^{\prime}\not\in P
, we find that

\displaystyle(M\cdot N+\mathbf{1})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot N(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})+[q=q^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})+[q=q^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=(M\cdot M^{*}+\mathbf{1})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=N(q,q^{\prime})

In the second step, we use
N\leq M^{*}
; after all, if
q,q^{\prime}\in P
then
N(q,q^{\prime})=(M_{P})^{*}(q,q^{\prime})\leq(M^{*})_{P}(q,q^{\prime})=M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})
(by the derivation above), and if
q\not\in P
or
q^{\prime}\not\in P
, then
N(q,q^{\prime})=M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})
by definition.
∎

Because of
Lemma

5.13
, we can write
M_{P}^{*}
instead of
(M_{P})^{*}
or
(M^{*})_{P}
without ambiguity, provided that
P
satisfies the condition specified.
With this in mind, we can now verify the first equation.

Lemma 5.14
.

If
M\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
is locally finite, then
\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*}=M^{*}
.

Proof.

We prove only that
\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*}\leq M^{*}
; the converse inequality follows from the fixpoint rule for matrices, proved in
Lemma

5.15
without relying on the claim at hand.
It thus remains to show that for
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
, we have that
(\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*})(q,q^{\prime})\leqq M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})
.

When
q^{\prime}\not\in R_{M}(q)
, we have that
M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=0
by definition.
Fortunately, this also means that
q^{\prime}\neq q
, and that
q^{\prime}\not\in R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})
for any
q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)
— after all, if either were the case, then
q^{\prime}\in R_{M}(q)
by definition of
R_{M}
.
This then means that
(\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*})(q,q^{\prime})=0
as well.

We thus focus on the case where
q^{\prime}\in R_{M}(q)
, and derive:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=[q=q^{\prime}]+\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q}M(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=[q=q^{\prime}]+\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)}M_{R_{M}(q)}(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=[q=q^{\prime}]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)\\
\text{s.t. $q^{\prime}\in R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})$}\end{subarray}}M_{R_{M}(q)}(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot(M_{R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})})^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq[q=q^{\prime}]+\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)}M_{R_{M}(q)}(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot(M_{R_{M}(q)})^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

(†)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}+M_{R_{M}(q)}\cdot(M_{R_{M}(q)})^{*})(q,q^{\prime})=(M_{R_{M}(q)})^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})

In the step marked with (†), we use that because
q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)
, it holds that
R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})\subseteq R_{M}(q)
, so if
q^{\prime\prime}\in R_{M}(q)
with
q^{\prime}\in R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})
, then

\displaystyle(M_{R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})})^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=((M_{R_{M}(q)})_{R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})})^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=((M_{R_{M}(q)})^{*})_{R_{M}(q^{\prime\prime})}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

(
Lemma

5.13
)

\displaystyle=(M_{R_{M}(q)})^{*}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

∎

We move on to the proof of the left fixpoint rule: i.e., that
B+MS\leq S
implies
M^{*}B\leq S
.
As before, we instead prove a variant of the claim for vectors.
The generalisation to matrices then follows.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
M\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
be locally finite, and let
b,s\colon Q\to\mathcal{K}
.
If
b+M\cdot s\leq s
, then
M^{*}\cdot b\leq s
as well.

Proof.

To prove that
M^{*}\cdot b\leq s
, we should show that for every
q\in Q
,

\sum_{q^{\prime}\in Q}M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})\cdot b(q^{\prime})\leq s(q)

For all
q^{\prime}\not\in R_{M}(q)
we have
M^{*}(q,q^{\prime})=0\leq s(q)
.
So the above is equivalent to showing that for all
q\in Q
, we have that

\sum_{q^{\prime}\in R_{M}(q)}(M_{R_{M}(q)})^{*}(q,q^{\prime})\cdot b_{R_{M}(q)}(q^{\prime})\leq s_{R_{M}(q)}(q)

which is true if for
q\in Q
, we have that
(M_{R_{M}(q)})^{*}\cdot b_{R_{M}(q)}\leq s_{R_{M}(q)}
.
Since this is an inequation of
finite
vectors, the latter holds when
b_{R_{M}(q)}+M_{R_{M}(q)}\cdot s_{R_{M}(q)}\leq s_{R_{M}(q)}
(cf.
Lemma

5.5
).
This follows from our assumption that
b+M\cdot s\leq s
by restricting both sides to
R_{M}(q)
.
∎

The right-handed laws for
M^{*}
, i.e., that (1)
M^{*}\cdot M+\textbf{1}=M^{*}
, (2) and if
B+S\cdot M\leq S
then
B\cdot M^{*}\leq S
, can be derived in the same way we derived these for the finite case — see
5.6
.

This concludes our study of the laws of Kleene algebra for matrices indexed by infinite sets; we will leverage these in the sections to come.

5.2
Soundness

For the left to right implication of
Theorem

5.1
(soundness), it is enough to prove that
\equiv
is a bisimulation relation — after all, since
\leftrightarroweq
is the largest bisimulation, this would imply that
\equiv
is a subset of
\leftrightarroweq
.
This will have as consequence that the Kleene algebra axiomatization of regular
expressions is sound: for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{Exp}
, if
e_{1}\equiv e_{2}
then

e_{1}\leftrightarroweq e_{2}
.

Theorem 5.16

(Soundness)
.

Provable equivalence is a bisimulation on
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
, that is, for every
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{Exp}
, if
e_{1}\equiv e_{2}
then

o(e_{1})=o(e_{2})\text{ and for all $\mathtt{a}\in A$, }(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}

Proof.

By induction on the length of derivations for
e_{1}\equiv e_{2}
.

For the base cases, i.e., all but the last two equations in
Definition

2.16
, everything follows from unwinding the definitions of
o
and
t
.
We show the proof for the equation
e_{1}+e_{2}\equiv e_{2}+e_{1}
:

\displaystyle o(e_{1}+e_{2})

\displaystyle=o(e_{1})\vee o(e_{2})=o(e_{2}+e_{1})

\displaystyle(e_{1}+e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}+(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}+(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv(e_{2}+e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}

For good measure, here is the proof for
ee^{*}+1\equiv e^{*}
:

\displaystyle o(ee^{*}+1)

\displaystyle=o(ee^{*})\vee o(1)=o(ee^{*})\vee 1=1=o(e^{*})

\displaystyle(ee^{*}+1)_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv(ee^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+1_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv e_{\mathtt{a}}e^{*}+{o(e)}(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+0

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}e_{\mathtt{a}}e^{*}+0(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+0&\text{if }o(e)=0\\
e_{\mathtt{a}}e^{*}+1(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+0(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+0&\text{if }o(e)=0\\
(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+1(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}+0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\equiv(e^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}

In the inductive step, we illustrate the case where
e_{2}^{*}e_{1}\leqq f
given
e_{1}+e_{2}f\leqq f
; the other case is proved precisely in the same way.
Suppose we have just derived
e_{2}^{*}e_{1}\leqq f
, using
e_{1}+e_{2}f\leqq f
as a premise.
Our induction hypothesis is the claim applied to this premise, so
o(e_{1}+e_{2}f)\leq o(f)
and
(e_{1}+e_{2}f)_{\mathtt{a}}\leqq f_{\mathtt{a}}
.
We want to prove that
o(e_{2}^{*}e_{1})\leq o(f)
and
(e_{2}^{*}e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}\leqq f_{\mathtt{a}}
.
The first inequality simplifies to
o(e_{1})\leq o(f)
, which follows from the first part of the induction hypothesis.
For the second inequality, we derive as follows:

\displaystyle(e_{2}^{*}e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle=(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}e_{2}^{*}e_{1}+(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\leqq(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}f+(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}

(
e_{2}^{*}e_{1}\leqq f
)

\displaystyle\leqq(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}f+{o(e_{2})}f_{\mathtt{a}}+(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv(e_{2}f)_{\mathtt{a}}+(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv(e_{1}+e_{2}f)_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\leqq f_{\mathtt{a}}

(induction hypothesis)

This completes the proof.
∎

5.3
The road to completeness

We now turn our attention to the central result of this chapter, namely
completeness
— the converse direction of
Theorem

5.1
.
Our proof of this fact will involve notions from coalgebra, discussed in
Chapter

3
and the blue remarks in
Chapter

4
.
Readers who skipped those parts are invited read them first, before returning back here.

Soundness (Theorem
5.16
) tells us that
\equiv
is a bisimulation on
\mathsf{Exp}
.

Lemma

3.19
then gives us a coalgebra

<\overline{o},\overline{t}>\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to 2\times(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv})^{A}

on
\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
such that the quotient map
[-]\colon\mathsf{Exp}\to\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
given by
[e]=\{e^{\prime}\mid e\equiv e^{\prime}\}
is a coalgebra homomorphism.
In concrete terms,
\overline{o}
and
\overline{t}
work as expected: given
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
,
\overline{o}([e])=o(e)
and
\overline{t}([e])(\mathtt{a})=[e_{\mathtt{a}}]
.

The proof of completeness that follows is inspired by a proof due to Jacobs
[
26
]
, which can be seen as a coalgebraic
review of Kozen’s proof
[
8
]
, though we present some lemmas (and respective proofs) slightly differently.
We start with an overview of all the necessary steps.

Step
1

First, let
L\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to 2^{A^{*}}
be the final homomorphism, and let
(I,<o_{I},t_{I}>)
be the
D
-coalgebra obtained by quotienting
(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
using
\mathsf{Ker}(L)
, per
Lemma

3.19
.
By
Lemma

3.29
, this brings us the situation below, with
q\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to I
being the quotient map, and
m
being the injective homomorphism from
(I,<o_{I},t_{I}>)
to
(2^{A^{*}},<o_{L},t_{L}>)
.

\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
I
2^{A^{*}}
2\times(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv})^{A}
2\times I^{A}
2\times(2^{A^{*}})^{A}
q
<\overline{o},\overline{t}>
D(q)
<o_{I},t_{I}>
m
D(m)
<o_{L},t_{L}>
L

Step
2

We prove that
q
is a bijection.
The key idea is that
(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
and
(I,<o_{I},t_{I}>)
are final among automata where the reach of each state is finite.
By
Proposition

4.28
,
(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
is such a coalgebra.

We prove that
(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
is final using three intermediate steps:

(
Lemma

5.18
)

For any locally finite automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
, there exists a homomorphism
\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{S}\colon(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)\to(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
.

(
Lemma

5.19
)

If
f
is a homomorphism from
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
to
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
and both are locally finite, then it holds that
\lceil\,f(s)\,\rceil_{T}=\lceil\,s\,\rceil_{S}
.

(
Lemma

5.21
)

The homomorphism
\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}
is the
identity.

These three lemmas together imply that for any locally finite automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
, there exists a
unique
coalgebra homomorphism

\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{S}\colon(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)\to(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)

Hence,
(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
is final among locally finite coalgebras.

With these facts in hand, the finality of
(I,<o_{I},t_{I}>)
is easy by comparison.
We just need to observe the following two properties:

(i)

For any locally finite automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
, we can construct a coalgebra homomorphism into
(I,<o_{I},t_{I}>)
as follows:

(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)\xrightarrow{\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{S}}(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)\xrightarrow{q}(I,o_{I},t_{I}>)

Here,
\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{S}
is as in the statement of
Lemma

5.18
, and
q
is the quotient morphism from the diagram above.

(ii)

If there exist two homomorphisms
f,g\colon(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)\to(I,<o_{I},t_{I}>)
, then
f=g
.
After all, by finality of
2^{A^{*}}
, we know that, for any
s\in S
,
L(s)=m\circ g(s)
and
L(s)=m\circ f(s)
.
Hence, we have that
m\circ f=m\circ g
and, since
m
is injective,
f=g
.

Step
3

From
1

and
2

,
q\colon(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)\to(I,<o_{I},t_{I}>)
is an isomorphism, and hence injective.
This makes
L=m\circ q\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to 2^{A^{*}}
injective, which is the key to prove completeness.

Theorem 5.17

(Completeness)
.

If
e_{1}\leftrightarroweq e_{2}
then
e_{1}\equiv e_{2}
.

Proof.

For all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{Exp}
, if
e_{1}\leftrightarroweq e_{2}
then they are mapped into the same element in the final coalgebra:
L(e_{1})=L(e_{2})
.
Since
[-]
is a homomorphism,
L([e_{1}])=L([e_{2}])
, where we use
L
to denote the map into the final coalgebra both from
\mathsf{Exp}
and
\mathcal{\mathsf{Exp}}/_{\equiv}
.
By
3

,
L
is injective and thus
[e_{1}]=[e_{2}]
, that is, by definition of
[-]
,
e_{1}\equiv e_{2}
.
∎

5.4
Filling in the details

It remains to prove the properties of
\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
alluded to above.
We start by constructing the desired map, and showing that it is a homomorphism.
In the following, we work with
Q
-matrices over
\mathsf{Exp}
; it should be clear that the Kleene star of a finite
Q
-matrix can still be defined syntactically; moreover, we write
M\equiv N
for matrices over
\mathsf{Exp}
to mean that
M
and
N
are pointwise equivalent (and similarly for vectors).

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
be a locally finite automaton.
There exists a coalgebra homomorphism
\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{S}\colon(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)\to(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
.

Proof.

Let
M_{S}\colon Q\times Q\to\mathsf{Exp}
be the transition matrix of
S
, given by

M_{S}(q,q^{\prime})=\sum\{\mathtt{a}\in A\mid t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{a})=q^{\prime}\}

Because
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
is locally finite, so is
M_{S}
.

If we view
o_{S}\colon Q\to 2
as a vector of expressions, then
M_{S}^{*}o_{S}
is another vector of expressions.
We choose
\lceil\,q\,\rceil_{S}=[(M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q)]
.
To see that
\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{S}
is a homomorphism, we must prove two equalities:

\overline{o}(\lceil\,q\,\rceil_{S})=o_{S}(q)\and\overline{t}(\lceil\,q\,\rceil_{S})(\mathtt{a})=\lceil\,t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{a})\,\rceil_{S}

For the first equality, we derive as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{o}(\lceil\,q\,\rceil_{S})

\displaystyle=o((M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q))

\displaystyle=o(o_{S}(q)+(M_{S}\cdot M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q))

\displaystyle=o(o_{S}(q))\vee o((M_{S}\cdot M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q))

\displaystyle=o(o_{S}(q))\vee\bigvee_{q^{\prime}}o(M_{S}(q,q^{\prime}))\wedge o((M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=o(o_{S}(q))

\displaystyle=o_{S}(q)

where the second-to-last step holds because
o(M_{S}(q,q^{\prime}))=0
.

For the second equality, we start by noting the following:

\displaystyle(M_{S}^{*}o_{S}(q))_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv(o_{S}(q)+(M_{S}\cdot M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q))_{\mathtt{a}}

(
Lemma

5.14
)

\displaystyle\equiv\Bigl(\sum_{q^{\prime}}M_{S}(q,q^{\prime})(M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q^{\prime})\Bigr)_{\mathtt{a}}

(
o_{S}(q)_{\mathtt{a}}=0
)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{q^{\prime}}\Bigl(\Bigl(\sum\{\mathtt{b}\in A\mid t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{b})=q^{\prime}\}\Bigr)\cdot(M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q^{\prime})\Bigr)_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum\{(\mathtt{b}\cdot(M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{b})))_{\mathtt{a}}\mid\mathtt{b}\in A\}

\displaystyle\equiv(M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{a}))

In the last step, we use that for
\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\in A
and
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
, we have that
(\mathtt{b}\cdot e)_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv e
when
\mathtt{a}=\mathtt{b}
, and
(\mathtt{b}\cdot e)_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv 0
otherwise.
We then conclude:

\overline{t}(\lceil\,q\,\rceil_{S})(\mathtt{a})=[((M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(q))_{\mathtt{a}}]=[(M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{a}))]=\lceil\,t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{a})\,\rceil\qed

Also, homomorphisms from locally finite automata to
(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},\overline{o},\overline{t})
commute with homomorphisms among locally finite automata.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
and
(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
be locally finite.
For any homomorphism
f\colon(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)\to(T,<o_{T},t_{T}>)
,
\lceil\,f(s)\,\rceil_{T}=\lceil\,s\,\rceil_{S}
.

Proof.

The idea behind this proof is to encode
f
in an
S
-by-
T
(rectangular) matrix, and exploit properties of matrix multiplication
[
26
]
.
In the proof below, we therefore expand matrix multiplication to rectangular matrices in the obvious way; it is routine to verify that the laws of Kleene algebra still apply to well-defined expressions.

Let
M_{f}\colon S\times T\to\mathsf{Exp}
be given by
M_{f}(q,q^{\prime})=[f(q)=q^{\prime}]
.
We start by claiming that
M_{f}\cdot M_{T}\equiv M_{S}\cdot M_{f}
.
To see this, derive as follows:

\displaystyle(M_{f}\cdot M_{T})(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}}M_{f}(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M_{T}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\equiv M_{T}(f(q),q^{\prime})

(def.
M_{f}
)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum\{\mathtt{a}\in A\mid t_{T}(f(q))(\mathtt{a})=q^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum\{\mathtt{a}\in A\mid f(t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{a}))=q^{\prime}\}

(homomorphism)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}}\Bigl\{\sum\{\mathtt{a}\mid t_{S}(q)(\mathtt{a})=q^{\prime\prime}\}\mid f(q^{\prime\prime})=q^{\prime}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}}M_{S}(q,q^{\prime\prime})\cdot M_{f}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\equiv(M_{S}\cdot M_{f})(q,q^{\prime})

We can thus conclude from the fact that
M_{f}\cdot M_{T}\equiv M_{S}\cdot M_{f}
that
M_{f}\cdot M_{T}^{*}\equiv M_{S}^{*}\cdot M_{f}
(see
5.3
).
Next, we claim that
M_{f}\cdot o_{T}\equiv o_{S}
; this is easier to show:

(M_{f}\cdot o_{T})(q)\equiv\sum_{q^{\prime}}M_{f}(q,q^{\prime})o_{T}(q^{\prime})\equiv o_{T}(f(q))=o_{S}(q)

Putting this together, we can derive that

\displaystyle\lceil\,f(s)\,\rceil_{T}

\displaystyle=[(M_{T}^{*}\cdot o_{T})(f(s))]

\displaystyle=[(M_{f}\cdot M_{T}^{*}\cdot o_{T})(s)]

\displaystyle=[(M_{S}^{*}\cdot M_{f}\cdot o_{T})(s)]

\displaystyle=[(M_{S}^{*}\cdot o_{S})(s)]

\displaystyle=\lceil\,s\,\rceil_{S}

∎

To place the final piece of the puzzle, we need the following property of
\lceil\,-\,\rceil
; the proof is left as
5.11
.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
e,f\in\mathsf{Exp}
.
If
e\leqq f
, then
\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}
.

With this in hand, we can now prove the last lemma, which says that
\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\colon(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)\to(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
acts as the identity.

Lemma 5.21
.

For all
e\in\mathsf{Exp}
, it holds that
\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}=[e]
.

Proof.

In the proof below, we treat
\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
as a Kleene algebra, extending the operators on
\mathsf{Exp}
to the equivalence classes; for instance,
[f]+[g]=[f+g]
.
This is well-defined because
\equiv
is a congruence with respect to all operators.
To prevent some clutter, we will treat
0,1\in 2
as equivalence classes of expressions.
We will also write
[f]\leq[g]
when
f\leqq g
.
Note that this makes
\leq
a partial order on
\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
.
To prove the claim, it now suffices to show that
\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}([e])\leq[e]
and
[e]\leq\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}([e])

To show that
\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq[e]
, we can use
\mathsf{id}\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
as a vector.
If
M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}
is the matrix corresponding to
\overline{t}\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to{(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv})}^{A}
, and we think of
\overline{o}\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to 2
as a vector, then we can derive as follows:

\displaystyle(M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\cdot\mathsf{id}+\overline{o})([e])

\displaystyle=\sum_{[f]}M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}([e],[f])\cdot[f]+\overline{o}([e])

\displaystyle=\sum_{[f]}\sum_{e_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv f}[\mathtt{a}\cdot f]+o(e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathtt{a}}[\mathtt{a}\cdot e_{\mathtt{a}}]+o(e)

\displaystyle=[e]

(
Theorem

4.3
)

It then follows that
\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}=[(M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}^{*}\cdot\overline{o})([e])]\leq[e]
.

In the other direction, we prove that for
e,f\in\mathsf{Exp}
, we have that
[e]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[e\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}
.
This implies the main claim, because

[e]=[e]\cdot[1]\leq[e]\cdot\lceil\,[1]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[e\cdot 1]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}=\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

The proof proceeds by induction on
e
, while still quantifying over all
f\in\mathsf{Exp}
.
In the base, there are three cases to consider:

•

If
e=0
, then the claim is trivial, because we have

[0]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}=[0]\leq\lceil\,[0\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

•

If
e=1
, then the claim also trivializes, because

[1]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}=\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}=\lceil\,[1\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

•

If
e=\mathtt{a}
for some
\mathtt{a}\in A
, then note that
[(\mathtt{a}\cdot f)_{\mathtt{a}}]=[f]
, and so
[\mathtt{a}]\leq M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}([\mathtt{a}\cdot f],[f])
.
With this in mind, we can derive

\displaystyle[\mathtt{a}]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

\displaystyle=[\mathtt{a}]\cdot(M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}^{*}\cdot\overline{o})([f])

\displaystyle\leq M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}([\mathtt{a}\cdot f],[f])\cdot(M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}^{*}\cdot\overline{o})([f])

\displaystyle\leq(M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}^{*}\cdot\overline{o})([\mathtt{a}\cdot f])

\displaystyle=\lceil\,[\mathtt{a}\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

For the inductive step, there are three more cases.

•

If
e=e_{1}+e_{2}
, then by induction we know that for
i\in\{1,2\}
:

[e_{i}]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[e_{i}\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

We can then derive that

\displaystyle[e]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

\displaystyle=[e_{1}]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}+[e_{2}]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

\displaystyle\leq\lceil\,[e_{1}\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}+\lceil\,[e_{2}\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

(IH)

\displaystyle\leq\lceil\,[e\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

(
Lemma

5.20
)

•

If
e=e_{1}\cdot e_{2}
, then the following hold for all
f\in\mathsf{Exp}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
:

[e_{i}]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[e_{i}\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

With these facts in hand, we can then derive for any
f\in\mathsf{Exp}
that

\displaystyle[e]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

\displaystyle=[e_{1}]\cdot([e_{2}]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}})

\displaystyle\leq[e_{1}]\cdot\lceil\,[e_{2}\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

(IH)

\displaystyle\leq\lceil\,[e_{1}\cdot(e_{2}\cdot f)]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

(IH)

\displaystyle=\lceil\,[e\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

•

If
e=e_{1}^{*}
, then we derive as follows:

\displaystyle[e_{1}]\cdot\lceil\,[e\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}+\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\lceil\,[e_{1}\cdot(e\cdot f)]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}+\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

(IH)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\lceil\,[e\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}

(
Lemma

5.20
)

By the fixpoint rule, the above then lets us derive that

[e]\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}=[e_{1}]^{*}\cdot\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[e\cdot f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\qed

5.5
Leveraging completeness

Completeness now enables the use of coinduction to prove that expressions are provably equivalent using the axioms of Kleene algebra.
All we have to do to show that
e\equiv f
is argue that that
L(e)=L(f)
.
Fortunately, the latter is decidable, by
Theorems

4.22
and
4.29
.

We could alternatively try to construct a bisimulation relating
e
to
f
on
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
directly.
Unfortunately, since the latter automaton is infinite (and not even locally finite!) such a bisimulation may become cumbersome to describe.
However, it turns out that we do not always need to explicitly specify the whole bisimulation — in many cases, describing a fragment is enough.
Such a fragment is commonly called a “bisimulation up-to
X
”, where
X
is some technique that completes the fragment into a true bisimulation.
We will now briefly discuss one instance of this technique, called
bisimulation up-to congruence

[
27
]
.

Definition 5.22
.

Let
R\subseteq\mathsf{Exp}^{2}
.
We write
\equiv_{R}
for the
congruence closure
of
R
, which is the smallest congruence on
\mathsf{Exp}
that contains
R
and
\equiv
.
We say
R
is a bisimulation
up-to congruence
when, for all
e\mathrel{R}f
, we have that
o(e)=o(f)
, and moreover
e_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv_{R}f_{\mathtt{a}}
for all
\mathtt{a}\in A
.

Note how a bisimulation up-to congruence is not
quite
a bisimulation in
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
: the second condition says that
e_{\mathtt{a}}
and
f_{\mathtt{a}}
are related by
\equiv_{R}
rather than
R
itself.
However, bisimulations up-to congruence give rise to bisimulations rather directly.

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
R\subseteq\mathsf{Exp}^{2}
.
If
R
is a bisimulation up-to congruence, then
\equiv_{R}
is a bisimulation on
(\mathsf{Exp},<o,t>)
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the construction of
\equiv_{R}
.
In the base,
e\equiv_{R}f
because either
e\mathrel{R}f
or
e\equiv f
.
In either case, we have that
o(e)=o(f)
and
e_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv_{R}f_{\mathtt{a}}
— either by the premise that
R
is a bisimulation up-to congruence, or the arguments from
Theorem

5.16
.

For the inductive step, there are several cases to consider.
Since they are all similar, we treat the one for
+
; here,
e\equiv_{R}f
because
e=e_{1}+e_{2}
and
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
, where
e_{i}\equiv_{R}f_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
In that case
o(e_{i})=o(f_{i})
and
(e_{i})_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv_{R}(f_{i})_{\mathtt{a}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
by induction.
This then tells us that
o(e)=o(f)
and
e_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv_{R}f_{\mathtt{a}}
, thus completing the proof.
∎

The upshot is that, to demonstrate bisimilarity, all we need to do is come up with a bisimulation up-to congruence.

Example 5.24
.

Let us illustrate the power of bisimulations up-to congruence by proving the denesting rule
(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\equiv(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}
(
2.6
), by proposing the following relation as a bisimulation up-to congruence:

R=\{<(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*},(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}>,<(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*},(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}+\mathtt{a}^{*}>\}

We can verify this claim for the first pair, as follows:

\displaystyle o((\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*})

\displaystyle=1=o((\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*})

\displaystyle((\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathrel{R}(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}+\mathtt{a}^{*}\equiv((\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle((\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*})_{\mathtt{b}}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathrel{R}(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}\equiv((\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*})_{\mathtt{b}}

For the second pair, our derivation goes like this:

\displaystyle o((\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*})

\displaystyle=1=o((\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}+\mathtt{a}^{*})

\displaystyle((\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathrel{R}(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}+\mathtt{a}^{*}\equiv((\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}+\mathtt{a}^{*})_{\mathtt{a}}

\displaystyle((\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*})_{\mathtt{b}}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathrel{R}(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}\equiv((\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})(\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}^{*}+\mathtt{a}^{*})_{\mathtt{b}}

Together, this shows that
R
is a bisimulation up-to congruence.

Example 5.25
.

Consider the following relation:

R=\{<(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*},\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}+\mathtt{b}^{*}>,<\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*},(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}>\}

This is a bisimulation up-to congruence witnessing that
(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}+\mathtt{a}^{*}
.
We leave checking this as
5.12
.

In comparison, the algebraic proof of the same equivalence requires a bit more ingenuity, using the denesting rule (left as
2.6
) and the slide rule (
Lemma

2.24
):

\displaystyle(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{b}^{*}

(denesting rule)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*})^{*}

(slide rule)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*})^{*}+1)

(
e^{*}\equiv 1+ee^{*}
)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*})^{*}+\mathtt{b}^{*}

(distributivity and
e1\equiv e
)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}+\mathtt{b}^{*}

(slide rule)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}+\mathtt{b}^{*}

(denesting rule)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}(\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})^{*}\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}^{*}+\mathtt{b}^{*}

(commutativity of
+
)

5.6
Exercises

5.1.

Let
Q
be a finite set, and let
X
,
Y
and
Z
be
Q
-matrices.
Prove the left-distributivity claimed in
Lemma

5.3
, namely that

X\cdot(Y+Z)=X\cdot Y+X\cdot Z

5.2.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a Kleene algebra, and let
M\colon Q\times Q\to\mathcal{K}
.

(a)

Show that if
M
is finite and
q\in Q
, then
R_{M}(q)=R_{M^{*}}(q)
.

Hint: use that
M^{*}=\mathbf{1}+M\cdot M^{*}
.

(b)

Show the same property, assuming that
M
is locally finite.

5.3.

Let
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
be finite sets, let
M
be a
Q_{0}
-by-
Q_{1}
matrix, let
X
be a
Q_{1}
-matrix, and let
Y
be a
Q_{0}
-matrix.

Show that
M\cdot X=Y\cdot M
implies
M\cdot X^{*}=Y^{*}\cdot M
.

Hint: if you’re stuck, suppose
e,f,g\in\mathsf{Exp}
with
e\cdot f\equiv g\cdot e
, and prove that
e\cdot f^{*}\equiv g^{*}\cdot e
(cf.
2.8
).
The structure of this proof can be adapted to your needs.

5.4.

In this exercise, we will complete the proof of
Lemma

5.4
.
First, let us recall the setup there.
We were given a finite
Q
-matrix
M
and a
Q
-vector
b
, and set out to construct a
Q
-vector
s
such that
b+M\cdot s\leq s
.
Additionally, we needed to show that if
t
is a
Q
-vector and
z\in\mathsf{Exp}
such that
b\fatsemi z+M\cdot t\leq t
, then
s\fatsemi z\leq t
.

In the inductive case, we assumed that this was possible for strictly smaller matrices and vectors.
We then chose a
p\in Q
, and set
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\{p\}
, and constructed a
Q^{\prime}
-matrix
M^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
-vector
b
:

M^{\prime}(q,q^{\prime})=M(q,q^{\prime})+M(q,p)\cdot{M(p,p)}^{*}\cdot M(p,q^{\prime})\and b^{\prime}(q)=b(q)+M(q,p)\cdot{M(p,p)}^{*}\cdot b(p)

By induction, we then obtained a
Q^{\prime}
-vector
s^{\prime}
such that
b^{\prime}+M^{\prime}\cdot s^{\prime}
, and moreover if
t^{\prime}
is a
Q^{\prime}
-vector such that
b^{\prime}\fatsemi z+M^{\prime}\cdot t^{\prime}\leq t^{\prime}
, then
s^{\prime}\fatsemi z\leq t^{\prime}
.
We then extended
s^{\prime}
to obtain a
Q
-vector
s
by setting

s(q)=\begin{cases}s^{\prime}(q)&q\in Q^{\prime}\\
{M(p,p)}^{*}\cdot\left(b(p)+\sum_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}M(p,q^{\prime})\cdot s^{\prime}(q^{\prime})\right)&q=p\end{cases}

(a)

Show that with this choice of
s
, we have that
b+M\cdot s\leq s
.

(b)

Let
t
be a
Q
-vector and
z\in\mathsf{Exp}
, with
b\fatsemi z+M\cdot t\leq t
.
Show:

{M(p,p)}^{*}\cdot\Bigl(b(p)\cdot z+\sum_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}M(p,q^{\prime})\cdot t(q^{\prime})\Bigr)\leqq t(p)

(c)

Use the fact above as well as the induction hypothesis applied to the
Q^{\prime}
-vector
t^{\prime}
given by
t^{\prime}(q)=t(q)
to show that if
t
is a
Q
-vector and
z\in\mathsf{Exp}
with
b\fatsemi z+M\cdot t\leq t
, then
s\fatsemi z\leq t
.

to prove

5.6
.

B+S\cdot M\leq S

.

and

.

Conclude that

5.7.

and

.

Let

.

and

.
For both inclusions, you need to use facts about the star of a matrix.

5.9.

We consider some of the remaining cases of

5.16
:

(a)

Show that
o((e_{1}\cdot e_{2})\cdot e_{3})=o(e_{1}\cdot(e_{2}\cdot e_{3}))
and
((e_{1}\cdot e_{2})\cdot e_{3})_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv(e_{1}\cdot(e_{2}\cdot e_{3}))_{\mathtt{a}}
(one of the base cases).

(b)

Show that if
o(e_{1})=o(e_{2})
and
(e_{1})_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv(e_{2})_{\mathtt{a}}
, then
o(e_{1}\cdot f)=o(e_{2}\cdot f)
and
(e_{1}\cdot f)_{\mathtt{a}}\equiv(e_{2}\cdot f)_{\mathtt{a}}
(one of the inductive steps).

5.10.

Prove the claim on page

:

,

and

together imply that from any locally finite automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
there exists a
