\lceil\,-\,\rceil_{S}
to
(\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv},<\overline{o},\overline{t}>)
.

5.11.

In this exercise, we prove
Lemma

5.20
: if
e\leqq f
, then
\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}
.
First, we need the matrix
M_{+}
over
\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
given by
M_{+}([g],[h])=1
if
g\equiv h+f
, and
M_{+}([g],[h])=0
otherwise.
We also define the right-multiplication of a vector
b
by a matrix
M
over
Q
as another vector over
Q
, in the expected way:

(b\cdot M)(q)=\sum_{q^{\prime}}b(q^{\prime})\cdot M(q^{\prime},q)

This multiplication interacts well with other matrix-matrix and matrix-vector multiplications, so we can drop parentheses.

(a)

Prove that for any
b\colon\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}\to\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}
and any
g\in\mathsf{Exp}
we have that
(b\cdot M_{+})([g])=b([g+f])
.

(b)

Use the fixpoint laws to show that
M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}^{*}\cdot o\leq M_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}^{*}\cdot o\cdot M_{+}
.

(c)

Use the two facts above to conclude
\lceil\,[e]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}\leq\lceil\,[f]\,\rceil_{\mathsf{Exp}/_{\equiv}}
.

5.12.

Verify that the relation
R
as given in
Example

5.24
is a bisimulation up-to congruence.

Chapter 6
Kleene algebra with tests

Previous chapters studied Kleene Algebra and equational reasoning over regular expressions.
As we mentioned in the introduction, regular expressions offer a useful algebraic perspective to reason about imperative programs.
However, to more faithfully model imperative programs we need to precisely capture
control flow
structures such as conditionals and while loops.
Recall the two programs from the introduction.

while

a

and

b

do

p

while

a

do

q
;

while

a

and

b

do

p
;

while

a

do

if

b

then

p
;

else

q
;

In this chapter we want to show how we can encode and reason about equivalence of these programs using regular expressions in a way that the effects of control flow are taken as first class citizens.
The key piece missing in regular expressions is a distinguished notion of
test
that enables the conditional execution of program actions.
We will explain how one can develop a theory of regular expressions — retaining all the elements from the previous chapters — in which we can have both tests and program actions.
This will lead to the notion of
Kleene algebra with tests
(
\mathsf{KAT}
), an equational system that combines Kleene and Boolean algebra originally proposed by Kozen
[
28
]
.
Within
\mathsf{KAT}
one can model imperative programming constructs and assertions, which can then be used to analyze control flow
[
29
,
30
]
, or data flow
[
31
,
32
]
, or even verify compiler optimizations
[
33
]
.
More recently, a variant of
\mathsf{KAT}
called
\mathsf{NetKAT}
was introduced to model forwarding behavior of networks, and verify important properties, such as reachability and access control
[
34
]
.

In
\mathsf{KAT}
, Boolean subexpressions called tests can be used to model assertions, i.e., properties that must hold at a certain point in the program before it can continue.
Using these assertions, we can encode conditionals and loops in a format that we encountered before
[
28
]
:

\mathbf{while}\ a\ \mathbf{do}\ e\triangleq(ae)^{*}\overline{a}\and\mathbf{if}\ a\ \mathbf{then}\ e\ \mathbf{else}\ f\triangleq ae+\overline{a}f

Here,
a
is a test (asserting
a
is true), and
\overline{a}
is its negation.

This chapter elides formal proofs of the claims made; in most cases, this is because they are analogous to their counterparts for
\mathsf{KA}
.
In particular, the claims about
\mathsf{KAT}
automata in
Section

6.3
can be proved by instantiating the coalgebraic machinery from
Chapter

3
; we provide remarks with references to the appropriate results in some cases.

6.1
Syntax and Semantics

In this section we describe the syntax and semantics of
\mathsf{KAT}
, before treating both the language and relational interpretation of expressions.

In order to reason about common programming constructs such as if-then-else statements and while-loops, we need to add a notion of
tests
to our syntax.
Given a set
P
of (primitive) actions and a set
\mathsf{Bt}
of (primitive) tests, we define the set
\mathsf{BExp}
of Boolean tests by:

\mathsf{BExp}\ni a,b\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\in\mathsf{Bt}\mid a\cdot b\mid a+b\mid\overline{a}\mid\texttt{0}\mid\texttt{1}

We intend for
\cdot
to be read as conjunction (and),
+
as conjunction (or), and
\overline{\phantom{a}}
as negation (not);
0
and
1
represent the tests that are always false and always true, respectively.
The set of
\mathsf{KAT}
expressions is

\textsf{KExp}\ni e,f\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\in\textsf{P}\mid b\in\mathsf{BExp}\mid e\cdot f\mid e+f\mid e^{*}

For now, we can think of tests as “assertions” inside a program, which make the program fail in a way that undoes all earlier effects.
This explains, for example, the encoding of the
\mathbf{if}
-
\mathbf{then}
-
\mathbf{else}
statement above, which makes the program branch non-deterministically into two programs,
ae
and
\overline{a}f
; among these two, either
a
holds and the first branch survives, or
\overline{a}
holds and the second branch does.

6.1.1
Relational semantics

As our first semantics of
\mathsf{KAT}
expressions, we extend the relational semantics given for
\mathsf{KA}
in
Section

2.1.1
.
To this end, we must assign a relational semantics to tests; here, the idea is that each test is associated with a subset of the identity relation on states: if
(s,s)
is in the semantics of
a
, it means that
a
succeeds in state
s
, without changing the state.

We show that the language model is sound and complete for this interpretation.
Thus,
\mathsf{KAT}
equivalence implies program equivalence for any programming language with a suitable relational semantics.

Definition 6.1

(Relational semantics)
.

A (
\mathsf{KAT}
)
interpretation
(over a set
S
) consists of functions
\sigma\colon\textsf{P}\to 2^{S\times S}
and
\tau\colon\mathsf{Bt}\to 2^{S}
.
The
relational semantics
of a
\mathsf{KAT}
expression
e
with respect to
(\sigma,\tau)
, written
\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}\colon\textsf{KExp}\to 2^{S\times S}
, is defined inductively, as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{0}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{1}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{id}_{S}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{id}_{\tau({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}})}

\displaystyle\llbracket e+f\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}\cup\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle\llbracket\overline{a}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{At}\setminus\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle\llbracket e\cdot f\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}\circ\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle\llbracket e^{*}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}^{*}

Note how in the above, we make no distinction between tests (in
\mathsf{BExp}
) and full-fledged programs (in
KExp
) when giving the semantics of
+
and
\cdot
.
It is not too hard to check that for every
a\in\mathsf{BExp}
, it still holds that
\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}\subseteq\mathsf{id}_{S}
.
In fact, because relational composition coincides with intersection on subsets of the identity relation, we recover the expected interpretation of
\cdot
as conjunction, i.e.,
\llbracket a\cdot b\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}=\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}\cap\llbracket b\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}
.

Example 6.2
.

Consider the expression
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}
and the interpretation over
S=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
given by
\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})=\{(s_{1},s_{2})\}
,
\tau({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}})=\{s_{2}\}
, and
\tau({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}})=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
.
It is easy to see that
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}=\{(s_{2},s_{2})\}
,
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}=\{(s_{1},s_{1}),(s_{2},s_{2})\}
and
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}=\{(s_{1},s_{2})\}
.
In total, we obtain:

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}=\{(s_{2},s_{2}),(s_{1},s_{2})\}

6.1.2
Language semantics

Like
\mathsf{KA}
,
\mathsf{KAT}
comes equipped with a
language model

[
35
]
, which abstracts from the interpretation of the primitive programs and tests.
Instead of words, languages in this model are composed of
guarded strings

[
36
]
.
Like ordinary words, guarded strings model the possible traces of a program, but they also record which tests hold before the program starts, between actions, and when the program ends.

Formally, we define
atoms
as subsets of
\mathsf{Bt}
, and write
\mathsf{At}
for the set of atoms.
In the sequel it will be convenient to denote an atom in a condensed notation that makes explicit which tests are (not) part of the atom.
This is done by listing all of the primitive tests in some order, and putting a bar over the ones not included; for instance, when
\mathsf{Bt}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\}
, we will write
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}
for the atom
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}
for the atom
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\}
.

We can then define the set
GS
of
guarded strings
by
\textsf{GS}=(\mathsf{At}\cdot\textsf{P})^{*}\cdot\mathsf{At}
.
Given guarded strings
\alpha w\beta
and
\beta x\gamma
, we define their
fusion product
by
\alpha w\beta\diamond\beta x\gamma=\alpha w\beta x\gamma
.
Note that this is defined only when the inner atoms are the same.
We write
GL
for
2^{\textsf{GS}}
, i.e., the set of
guarded languages
.
Given
X,Y\subseteq\textsf{GS}
, we write
X\diamond Y
for the set containing all fusion products
x\diamond y
of
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, whenever these are defined.
Furthermore,
X^{(n)}
is defined inductively as
X^{(0)}=\mathsf{At}
and
X^{(n+1)}=X\diamond X^{(n)}
; this allows us to define
X^{(*)}
as
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}X^{(*)}
.

Definition 6.3

(Language semantics)
.

Each
\mathsf{KAT}
expression
e
denotes a set
\llbracket e\rrbracket
of guarded strings
defined inductively on the structure of
e
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{0}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{At}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\beta\mid\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{At}\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\mathsf{At}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\in\alpha\}

\displaystyle\llbracket e+f\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket\cup\llbracket f\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\overline{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{At}\setminus\llbracket a\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket e\cdot f\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket\diamond\llbracket f\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket e^{*}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket^{(*)}

A guarded language
L\subseteq\textsf{GS}
is
regular
if
L=\llbracket e\rrbracket
for some
e\in\textsf{KExp}
.

The definition of the semantics of sequential composition in terms of fusion product has an intuitive explanation: a trace modeled by
e\cdot f
is a trace of
e
that ended in some machine state
\alpha
, which then continues with a trace of
f
that starts in that same state
\alpha
.

Example 6.4
.

Consider the
\mathsf{KAT}
expression
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}
from
Example

6.2
.
We compute its language semantics as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\rrbracket\cup(\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}\rrbracket\diamond\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\mathsf{At}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\in\alpha\}\cup\left(\{\alpha\in\mathsf{At}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\in\alpha\}\diamond\{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\beta\mid\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{At}\}\right)

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\mathsf{At}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\in\alpha\}\cup\{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\beta\mid\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{At},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\in\alpha\}

Example 6.5
.

Now consider the expression
\mathbf{if}\ a\ \mathbf{then}\ e\ \mathbf{else}\ e
and note that its semantics can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{if}\ a\ \mathbf{then}\ e\ \mathbf{else}\ e\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket a\rrbracket\diamond\llbracket e\rrbracket\cup\llbracket\overline{a}\rrbracket\diamond\llbracket e\rrbracket

\displaystyle=(\llbracket a\rrbracket\cup\llbracket\overline{a}\rrbracket)\diamond\llbracket e\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{At}\diamond\llbracket e\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket e\rrbracket

This suggests that the language semantics equates
\mathbf{if}\ a\ \mathbf{then}\ e\ \mathbf{else}\ e
with
e
— and this should be no surprise:
e
is executed regardless of
a
.

Just like before, the language model abstracts the various relational interpretations in a sound and complete way
[
35
]
.
The proof is similar to that of
Theorem

2.12
, and is left as
6.7
.

Theorem 6.6
.

The language model is sound and complete for the relational model, in the following sense:

\llbracket e\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket\quad\iff\quad\forall\sigma,\tau.\ \llbracket e\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}=\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\sigma,\tau}

6.2
Reasoning

Next, we extend the reasoning principles used for
\mathsf{KA}
to
\mathsf{KAT}
.
The basic idea is to include all of the laws of Boolean algebra for the tests.

Definition 6.7

(Provable equivalence)
.

We define
\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}
as the smallest congruence on
KExp
that includes the laws from
Definition

2.16
, as well as the laws of
Boolean algebra
— that is, for all
a,b,c\in\mathsf{BExp}
:

a+\texttt{0}=a=a\cdot\texttt{1}\and a\cdot(b\cdot c)=(a\cdot b)\cdot c\and a+b=b+a\and a\cdot b=b\cdot a\and a+(b+c)=(a+b)+c\and a+(a\cdot b)=a=a\cdot(a+b)\and\texttt{0}=a\cdot\overline{a}\and\texttt{1}=a+\overline{a}\and a+(b\cdot c)=(a+b)\cdot(a+c)\and a\cdot(b+c)=(a\cdot b)+(a\cdot c)

To keep our notation simple, we will usually drop the subscript and write
\equiv
for
\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}
.
Conversely, we may use
\equiv_{\mathsf{KA}}
to signal that two
\mathsf{KAT}
expressions are equivalent by the laws of
\mathsf{KA}
alone.

As expected, the laws of
\mathsf{KAT}
are sound in the sense that if
e\equiv f
, then
\llbracket e\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket
.
We record this below; the proof is straightforward, by induction on the construction of
\equiv
, so we omit it.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
e,f\in\textsf{KExp}
.
If
e\equiv f
, then
\llbracket e\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket
.

Example 6.9

(Basic conditional laws)
.

We prove, using the axioms of Boolean and Kleene algebra, two familiar identities about conditionals.

\mathbf{if}\ a\ \mathbf{then}\ e\ \mathbf{else}\ e\equiv e\and\mathbf{if}\ a\ \mathbf{then}\ (\mathbf{if}\ \overline{a}\ \mathbf{then}\ e)\ \mathbf{else}\ f\equiv\mathbf{if}\ \overline{a}\ \mathbf{then}\ f

Using our encodings, the above laws translate to the following claims:

a{e}+\overline{a}e\equiv e\ \and a(\overline{a}e+a)+\overline{a}f\equiv\overline{a}f+a

These in turn can be proved as follows.
The first one is immediate:

\displaystyle a{e}+\overline{a}e

\displaystyle\equiv(a+\overline{a})e

(distributivity)

\displaystyle\equiv\texttt{1}e

(
a+\overline{a}\equiv\texttt{1}
)

\displaystyle\equiv e

(unit law)

The second one requires a few more steps, but all are quite simple:

\displaystyle a(\overline{a}e+a)+\overline{a}f

\displaystyle\equiv(a\overline{a})e+aa+\overline{a}f

(distributivity, associativity)

\displaystyle\equiv(a\overline{a})e+a+\overline{a}f

(
aa\equiv a
)

\displaystyle\equiv\texttt{0}e+a+\overline{a}f

(
a\overline{a}\equiv\texttt{0}
)

\displaystyle\equiv a+\overline{a}f

(unit laws)

\displaystyle\equiv\overline{a}f+a

(commutativity)

As before, these laws can be formulated algebraically, allowing us to speak of “a
\mathsf{KAT}
” as an algebra that satisfies the laws from
Definition

6.7
.

Definition 6.10

(
\mathsf{KAT}
)
.

A Kleene algebra with tests (
\mathsf{KAT}
) is a two-sorted structure
(A,B,+,\cdot,-^{*},\overline{\phantom{a}},0,1)
where
(A,+,\cdot,-^{*},0,1)
is a Kleene algebra and

(B,+,\cdot,\overline{\phantom{a}},0,1)
is a Boolean algebra.
In particular, the latter requirement tells us that
B
is closed under
+
and
\cdot
, and that the operators on
B
satisfy the laws from
Definition

6.7
.

Concrete examples of
\mathsf{KAT}
s include
(2^{\textsf{GS}},\cup,\diamond,{-}^{(*)},\overline{\phantom{a}},\emptyset,\mathsf{At})
, called the
\mathsf{KAT}
of guarded languages
, and
(2^{S\times S},\cup,\circ,{-}^{*},\overline{\phantom{a}},\emptyset,2^{\mathsf{id}_{S}})
for any set
S
, called the
\mathsf{KAT}
of relations on
S
. Note that in these two examples
\overline{\phantom{a}}
is just set complement.

6.3
Automata and Bisimulation

Now that we familiarized ourselves with how the syntax and semantics of
\mathsf{KA}
can be extended with tests, it is time to talk about the accompanying automata model.
Here too, the tweaks are relatively minor: instead of accepting words consisting of just letters, we need to recognize guarded words.
To achieve that, the
\mathsf{KAT}
automata will move from state to state while reading both an atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and a primitive letter
{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\in\textsf{P}
; acceptance of a word in each state is determined by the final atom.
If we formally encode these transitions and acceptance conditions, the model below arises naturally; it is a deterministic version of automata on guarded strings as introduced by
[
37
]
.

Definition 6.11

(
\mathsf{KAT}
automata
[
38
]
)
.

A
\mathsf{KAT}
automaton is a pair
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
where
o_{S}\colon S\to 2^{\mathsf{At}}
and
t_{S}\colon S\to S^{\mathsf{At}\times\textsf{P}}
.

Remark 6.12
.

Blue remarks such as this one make a return in this section, for those who read
Chapter

3
and are interested in the coalgebraic background.
In the case of the definition above, it should be clear that
\mathsf{KAT}
automata are
F
-coalgebras for

F(X)=2^{\mathsf{At}}\times X^{\mathsf{At}\times P}
.

s_{0}
s_{1}
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}
1
p
,
a
q
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}
1
p
,
1
q

Figure 6.1:
An example
\mathsf{KAT}
automaton on
\textsf{P}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}},{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}\}
and
\mathsf{Bt}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\}
.

Just like classical automata, we can draw
\mathsf{KAT}
automata as graphs, as in
Figure

6.1
.
Rather than labeling the transitions with single letters, we endow them with pairs of atoms and letters — i.e., for a pair of states
s
and
s^{\prime}
, we attach
\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}
to the arrow from
s
to
s^{\prime}
when
t_{S}(s)(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})=s^{\prime}
.
In lieu of accepting states the acceptance function is depicted by drawing double arrows (
\Rightarrow
) from each state to atoms that are accepted in that state — i.e., if
\alpha
is such that
o_{S}(\alpha)=1
, then
s
has a double arrow to
\alpha
.
To keep these drawings compact, we cluster atoms — e.g., a transition label
b
p
is shorthand for the labels
a
b
p
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}
; more formally, a label
b{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}
is shorthand for
\alpha_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}},\dots,\alpha_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}
where
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\leqq b
.

Given a
\mathsf{KAT}
automaton
(S,<o_{S},t_{S}>)
, the language of a state
s\in S
, also denoted
L(s)
, is guarded language defined inductively:

L(s)(\alpha)=o_{S}(\alpha)\and L(s)(\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}w)=L(t_{S}(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}))(w)

Informally, acceptance of a guarded string consisting of just an atom is determined by
o_{S}
, while for longer guarded strings this depends on whether the remainder of the string is accepted by the state reached after reading the first atom and letter.

Remark 6.13
.

The set of guarded languages
\textsf{GL}=2^{\textsf{GS}}
has an
F
-coalgebra structure
<o_{\textsf{GL}},t_{\textsf{GL}}>
given by

o_{\textsf{GL}}(L)(\alpha)=L(\alpha)\and t_{\textsf{GL}}(L)(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})=\{w\mid\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}w\in L\}

In fact,
(\textsf{GL},<o_{\textsf{GL}},t_{\textsf{GL}}>)
is the final
F
-coalgebra, and the map
L
discussed above, which sends each state of a given
F
-coalgebra to a guarded language in
GL
, is the unique
F
-coalgebra homomorphism from that
F
-coalgebra into the final
F
-coalgebra; one can prove this claim by replaying the arguments from
Theorem

4.14
.

6.3.1
Expressions to automata

We now have two formalisms to represent guarded languages:
\mathsf{KAT}
expressions and
\mathsf{KAT}
automata.
Unsurprisingly, there is a two-way correspondence between these, in the style of Kleene’s theorem: a guarded language is represented by a
\mathsf{KAT}
expression if and only if it is represented by a
\mathsf{KAT}
automaton.
For the purpose of this chapter, we limit ourselves to discussing the conversion from expressions to automata using Brzozowski derivatives; for the other direction, a matrix-based approach similar to the one in
Section

4.3.2
suffices.

Definition 6.14

(Brzozowski derivatives for
\mathsf{KAT}
expressions)
.

Given a
\mathsf{KAT}
expression
e\in\textsf{KExp}
, we define
E\colon\textsf{KExp}\to 2^{\mathsf{At}}
and
D\colon\textsf{KExp}\to\textsf{KExp}^{\mathsf{At}\times\textsf{P}}
by induction on the structure of
e
.
First,
E(e)
is given by:

\displaystyle E(\texttt{0})

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle E(\texttt{1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{At}

\displaystyle E({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle E({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}})

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\mathsf{At}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\in\alpha\}

\displaystyle E(e+f)

\displaystyle=E(e)\cup E(f)

\displaystyle E(\overline{b})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{At}\setminus E(b)

\displaystyle E(ef)

\displaystyle=E(e)\cap E(f)

\displaystyle E(e^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{At}

Next, we define
e_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}=D(e)(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}})
by

\begin{array}[]{llll}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}=\begin{cases}\texttt{1}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}={\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}\\
\texttt{0}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}\end{cases}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}=\texttt{0}&(ef)_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}=\begin{cases}e_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}f+f_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}&\text{if }\alpha\in E(e)\\
e_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}f&\text{if }\alpha\not\in E(e)\end{cases}\\[17.22217pt]
(e+f)_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}=e_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}+f_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}&&(e^{*})_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}=e_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}e^{*}\end{array}

Example 6.15
.

Let
\textsf{P}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\}
and
\mathsf{Bt}=\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}\}
.
If
e={{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}+{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}
, then

\displaystyle E(e)

\displaystyle=(E({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}})\cap E({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}))\cup(E({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}})\cap E({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}))

\displaystyle=(\{\alpha\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\in\alpha\}\cap\emptyset)\cup(\{\alpha\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\in\alpha\}\cap\emptyset)=\emptyset

Moreover, we can compute the values of
D(e)
as follows:

\displaystyle e_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}+({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}=\texttt{0}+({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}})=\texttt{0}+(\texttt{0}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\texttt{1})

\displaystyle e_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}+({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}=\texttt{0}+({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}_{{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}_{{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}})=\texttt{0}+(\texttt{0}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\texttt{1})

\displaystyle e_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}+({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}=\texttt{0}+{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}=\texttt{0}+\texttt{0}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}

\displaystyle e_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}+({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}=\texttt{0}+{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}=\texttt{0}+\texttt{0}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}

These derivatives make
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
a
\mathsf{KAT}
automaton.
The semantics of
\mathsf{KAT}
automata then gives us a map
L\colon\textsf{KExp}\to\textsf{GL}
, associating with each expression the language it accepts as a state of this automaton.
As expected, this map coincides with
\llbracket-\rrbracket\colon\textsf{KExp}\to\textsf{GL}
.

Theorem 6.16
.

For all
e\in\textsf{KExp}
, it holds that
L(e)=\llbracket e\rrbracket
.

Remark 6.17
.

Although the theorem above can be proved directly, a somewhat more elegant proof arises coalgebraically.
Note that, by finality,
L
is the unique
F
-coalgebra homomorphism that makes the following diagram commute:

KExp
2^{\textsf{GS}}
2^{\mathsf{At}}\times\textsf{KExp}^{\mathsf{At}\times\textsf{P}}
2^{\mathsf{At}}\times{(2^{\textsf{GS}})}^{\mathsf{At}\times\textsf{P}}
L
<E,D>
F(L)
<o_{\textsf{GL}},t_{\textsf{GL}}>

Thus, if we can show that
\llbracket-\rrbracket
is also an
F
-coalgebra homomorphism that fits in the same place, the claim follows.
In essence, this latter condition comes down proving that:

\llbracket e\rrbracket=E(e)\cup\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathsf{At},\ {\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\in\textsf{P}}\{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}w\mid w\in\llbracket e_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}\rrbracket\}

This follows from a version of
Theorem

4.3
for
\mathsf{KAT}
.

Of course, this only shows that there is a
\mathsf{KAT}
automaton for each expression; for this correspondence to be useful, we need to obtain a
finite

\mathsf{KAT}
automaton.
Fortunately, the same technique as before lets us tame
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
to obtain a finite automaton for each expression.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
\cong
be the congruence on
KExp
that equates terms up to associativity, commutativity and idempotence of
+
(see
Definition

4.25
).
There exists, for every
e\in\textsf{KExp}
, a finite set
\rho(e)\subseteq\textsf{KExp}
such that, in
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
,
e
reaches only states
\cong
-related to some element of
\rho(e)
.

Lemma 6.19
.

E
and
D
are well-defined up to
\cong
, i.e., if
e\cong f
then
E(e)=E(f)
, and for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\in\textsf{P}
we have that
e_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}\cong f_{\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}
.

Remark 6.20
.

The last lemma is tantamount to claiming that
\cong
is an
F
-bisimulation on the
F
-coalgebra
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
, and can be shown quite easily by induction on the construction of
\cong
.

Together, these two lemmas tell us that we can consider states in the Brzozowski automaton
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
up to
\cong
; with this convention, each state can reach only a finite number of other states that differ under
\cong
.
Thus, for a given
\mathsf{KAT}
expression
e\in\textsf{KExp}
, we can (lazily) generate a finite automaton just like we did for regular expressions.

Example 6.21
.

Let
P
,
\mathsf{Bt}
and
e
be as in
Example

6.15
.
Because for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, we have that

and

, we obtain the following automaton for
e

6.3.2
Decision procedure

It is not too hard to transpose the notion of bisimulation for automata (
Definition

4.17
) to
\mathsf{KAT}
automata; this yields the following.

Definition 6.22

(
\mathsf{KAT}
bisimulation)
.

Given
\mathsf{KAT}
automata
(S,<o_{X},t_{X}>)
and
(Y,<o_{Y},t_{Y}>)
we call
R\subseteq X\times Y
is a
bisimulation
if for all
(x,y)\in R
:

1.

o_{X}(x)(\alpha)=o_{Y}(y)(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, and

2.

for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\in\textsf{P}
,
(t_{X}(x)(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}),t_{Y}(y)(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}))\in R
.

We denote the largest bisimulation on
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
by
\leftrightarroweq
, and we can use it as a sound and complete proof principle for equivalence.

Theorem 6.23

(
\mathsf{KAT}
coinduction)
.

Let
e,f
be
\mathsf{KAT}
expressions.

e\leftrightarroweq f\iff\llbracket e\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket

Remark 6.24
.

It is not too hard to see that, in analogy with
4.18
, bisimulations the sense of
Definition

6.22
are precisely
F
-bisimulations when
\mathsf{KAT}
automata are regarded as coalgebras.
With that in mind, the forward direction of the claim above follows directly from
Lemma

3.20
, and the backward direction follows from
Corollary

3.27
provided we can show that
F
is kernel-compatible; the latter is not too hard to show.

Theorem

6.23
provides an alternative way to prove that two expressions are equivalent: build the automaton corresponding to each expression using Brzozowski derivatives and then check if they are bisimilar.
To make the latter decidable, we have to leverage
, and work with bisimulations up-to
\cong
.

Indeed, we can take this idea one step further, and work with bisimulations up-to
\equiv
, like we did in
Section

5.5
, but for
\mathsf{KAT}
.
Given a relation
R\subseteq\textsf{KExp}
, we can write
\equiv_{R}
or the smallest congruence on
KExp
containing both
R
and
\equiv
.
We now say that
R
is a
bisimulation up-to congruence
if it satisfies the following for all
e\mathrel{R}f
:

1.

E(e)(\alpha)=E(f)(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, and

2.

for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\in\textsf{P}
,
D(e)(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})\equiv_{R}D(f)(\alpha,{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})
.

The following generalization of
Lemma

5.23
then follows by analogy:

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
R\subseteq\textsf{KExp}^{2}
.
If
R
is a bisimulation up-to congruence, then
\equiv_{R}
is a bisimulation on
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
.

Thus, to show that
e\equiv f
, it suffices find a bisimulation up-to congruence
R
such that
e\mathrel{R}f
.
This is also necessary — it is not too hard to show that
\equiv
is a bisimulation on
(\textsf{KExp},<E,D>)
, which makes it a bisimulation up-to congruence (note that
{\equiv_{\equiv}}
coincides with
\equiv
); hence, if
e\equiv f
, then there exists a bisimulation up-to congruence relating
e
to
f
.
To demonstrate this technique, we will momentarily apply it to

({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}

which encode the second and the first program from the introduction.
By way of contrast, we first consider a purely algebraic derivation.

Bisimulation proof

We now repeat the same proof, but using a bisimulation up-to congruence.
First, we analyze the derivatives of the two expressions.
We start with
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
and observe that
E(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}})=\{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}\}
.
Moreover, many derivatives are equivalent to
0
; for instance, in the case of
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}
and

\displaystyle(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}})_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}}

works out as follows:

\displaystyle\phantom{=}(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}})_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}

\displaystyle=(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*})_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}{}+((\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}})_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}{}+(\texttt{0}+(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}})_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}})

\displaystyle=\texttt{0}+({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}})_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}}

\displaystyle=\texttt{0}+\texttt{1}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\texttt{0}

\displaystyle\equiv({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}

Thus,
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
and
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
are alike, in that (1) they are assigned the same value by
E
, (2) the same six derivatives are equivalent to
0
, and (3) they are equivalent to their remaining two derivatives.
All of this is sufficient to verify that the following is a bisimulation up-to congruence:

R=\{({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}})^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}},({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})^{*}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))^{*}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}})\}

Among the above, the second (bisimulation-based) approach required more text to describe, in spite of us eliding several of the derivations.
Still, whereas the first (algebraic) proof required some ingenuity in which lemmas to apply, it should be noted that all steps in the bisimulation-based approach could be mechanized, from the calculation of
E
and
D
to the constant-folding that occurs at the end of each derivation.
More generally, if one of the expressions has a derivation that simplifies to an expression not already seen, we can continue by analyzing the derivatives of that expression, and so on, until no new expressions remain;
Lemma

6.18
guarantees that this process terminates.
For automated reasoning, the bisimulation-based approach is therefore preferable.

6.4
Completeness, a sketch

For
\mathsf{KAT}
it was shown in
[
35
]
that there exists a similar result as we proved for
\mathsf{KA}
in the previous chapter — i.e., that semantic equivalence and provable equivalence coincide.
More formally, we have

Theorem 6.26

(Soundness and Completeness for
\mathsf{KAT}
)
.

For
e,f\in\textsf{KExp}
:

e\equiv f\Leftrightarrow\llbracket e\rrbracket=\llbracket f\rrbracket

The forward direction of this theorem (soundness) we had already seen in
Theorem

6.8
.
We finish the technical contents of this booklet by sketching the proof of the backwards claim (completeness).
However, rather than replaying the arguments from
Chapter

5
, we rely on a technique called
reduction
, which lets the completeness of
\mathsf{KA}
do the heavy lifting for us.
This idea relies the insight that expressions in
\mathsf{KAT}
are simply regular expressions over the alphabet
A=\textsf{P}\cup\mathsf{BExp}
.
1
1

1

Granted, this is an infinite alphabet, whereas we assumed beforehand that
A
was finite.
However, it should be clear that expressions over any alphabet will only use finitely many letters; hence, we can restrict our attention to the part of
A
that
is
used, and instantiate our earlier results to that finite subalphabet.

Thus, in the special case that
e
and
f
have the same regular language, completeness tells us that
e\equiv_{\mathsf{KA}}f
, and hence that
e\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}f
(since laws of
\mathsf{KA}
are laws of
\mathsf{KAT}
).
Obviously, this is not true for all
e
and
f
, and so the game becomes to massage them to obtain
\mathsf{KAT}
-equivalent expressions where we can apply this insight.

To further illustrate this idea, suppose for instance that
e={\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\overline{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}
and
f={\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}
.
In this case,
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KAT}}=\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KAT}}
, but

\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KA}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\overline{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}\}\neq\{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}},{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}\}=\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KA}}

Nevertheless, we can show that

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})+{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})

Furthermore, the expressions on the right-hand side above have the same regular language.
By completeness of
\mathsf{KA}
, they are therefore provably equivalent using
\equiv_{\mathsf{KA}}
(in fact, a proof is not too hard to work out manually in this case).
We conclude that
e\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}f
.

The approach for the two expressions above relies on substituting a test (e.g.,
\overline{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}
) with the sum of atoms obtained through
E
(e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}
).
In general, this transformation is sound by the laws of Boolean algebra, and hence also by the laws of
\mathsf{KAT}
: we have that for
b\in\mathsf{BExp}
,
b\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}\sum_{\alpha\leq b}\alpha
.
Such a substitution is therefore sound.

Unfortunately, this does not suffice in general for our larger aim.
Consider for instance
e=({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}})\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
and
f={\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
.
Simply substituting
a
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
with
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}
does not get us to our goal: the regular language semantics of
({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}+({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}}))(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})
contains the word
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\cdot\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}
(the
\cdot
marks the boundary between the “letters”
a
b
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}
), which is not found in the regular language obtained from
{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}+\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}})
.
However, if we distribute the multiplication in
e
, we get
{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
.
Here, the subexpression
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}}
is now a single test, which does not have any atoms below it and can therefore be replaced with
0
, the empty sum.
For the substitution to be effective, therefore, tests that are executed in sequence must also appear as a single (conjunctive) test.

Fortunately, this obstacle can also be overcome, and with some care it is possible to derive a translation
r\colon\textsf{KExp}\to\mathsf{Exp}

[
35
]
which satisfies the following two properties for all
e\in\textsf{KExp}
:

\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KAT}}=\llbracket r(e)\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KA}}\and e\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}r(e)

These two properties lead to a proof of
Theorem

6.26
that goes as follows.
First, take
e,f\in\textsf{KExp}
, and suppose
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KAT}}=\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KAT}}
.
The first property above tells us that
\llbracket r(e)\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KA}}=\llbracket r(f)\rrbracket_{\mathsf{KA}}
.
From the latter, it follows that
r(e)\equiv_{\mathsf{KA}}r(f)
by completeness, and hence also
r(e)\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}r(f)
.
Finally, since
e\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}r(e)
and
r(f)\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}f
by the second property, we can chain these equivalences to conclude that
e\equiv_{\mathsf{KAT}}f
.

This strategy for proving completeness is an instance of a more general technique, captured in a framework called
Kleene algebra with hypotheses

[
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
.
Intuitively, additional axioms are added to
\mathsf{KA}
, and completeness is established through completeness of
\mathsf{KA}
via a function satisfying similar properties to
r
.
The reduction is used to capture syntactically (i.e., on the level of expressions) the behaviour of the newly added axioms, such that the regular language semantics of the reduced terms (
r(e)
and
r(f)
above) coincides with the semantics of the
\mathsf{KA}
with additional axioms.
Indeed, in some cases it is possible to combine a reduction for one extension of
\mathsf{KA}
with a reduction for another extension, and thus obtain a reduction (and hence a completeness proof) for the combination of those extensions.
More pointers to further reading on this topic can be found in Chapter
7
.

6.5
Exercises

6.1.

Let
b\in\mathsf{BExp}
.
Prove that
b+b\equiv b
and
b\cdot b\equiv b
.

6.2.

In the following, let
b,c\in\mathsf{BExp}
.

(a)

Suppose
b+c\equiv\texttt{1}
and
b\cdot c\equiv\texttt{0}
.
Prove
b\equiv\overline{c}
.

(b)

Use the fact above to prove that
\overline{\overline{b}}\equiv b
.

(c)

Prove DeMorgan’s first law:
\overline{b+c}\equiv\overline{b}\cdot\overline{c}
.

(d)

Prove DeMorgan’s second law:
\overline{b\cdot c}\equiv\overline{b}+\overline{c}
.

6.3.

The axioms that we presented for Boolean algebra are somewhat redundant, in that we can prove some using the other laws.
In this exercise, we will explore this a bit more.
Below, let
b,c\in\mathsf{BExp}
.

(a)

Prove that
b+\texttt{1}\equiv\texttt{1}
and
b\cdot\texttt{0}\equiv\texttt{0}
, without using the absorption axioms
b+(b\cdot c)=b
or
b\cdot(b+c)=b
.

(b)

Use the facts derived above to prove the absorption axioms.

6.4.

We now look at some laws of Boolean algebra that can be proved
without the associativity axioms
b\cdot(c\cdot d)=(b\cdot c)\cdot d
and
b+(c+d)=(b+c)+d
; you are forbidden from using these laws in this exercise.

(a)

Prove that
b+(c\cdot d)\equiv b+(b+c)\cdot d
.

(b)

Prove that
b+(c+(b\cdot d))\equiv b+c
using the above.

(c)

Conclude that
b+(\overline{b}+c)\equiv\texttt{1}
.

6.5.

Let
b,c\in\mathsf{BExp}
and
e,f,g\in\textsf{KExp}
.

(a)

Show that it holds that
(b+e)^{*}\equiv e^{*}
.

(b)

Prove that
\mathbf{if}
-
\mathbf{then}
-
\mathbf{else}
is “skew-associative”, i.e.:

\displaystyle\mathbf{if}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ (\mathbf{if}\ c\ \mathbf{then}\ e\ \mathbf{else}\ f)\ \mathbf{else}\ g

\displaystyle\quad{}\equiv\mathbf{if}\ b\cdot c\ \mathbf{then}\ e\ \mathbf{else}\ (\mathbf{if}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ f\ \mathbf{else}\ g)

(c)

Suppose
\mathbf{if}\ b\ \mathbf{then}\ e\cdot f\ \mathbf{else}\ g\leqq f
.
Show that

(\mathbf{while}\ b\ \mathbf{do}\ e)\cdot g\leqq f

Hint: You need to use one of the star axioms.

6.6.

(a)

Show, using the axioms of
\mathsf{KAT}
, that the following programs are equivalent:
(\mathbf{while}\ ({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}})\ \mathbf{do}\ {\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})\cdot(\mathbf{if}\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}}\ \mathbf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt q}}\ \mathbf{else}\ {\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt r}})
and
(\mathbf{while}\ ({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt a}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textnormal{\bf\tt b}})\ \mathbf{do}\ {\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt p}})\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\textnormal{\bf\tt r}}
.

(b)

Show the above equivalence using an argument based on bisimulation up-to congruence.

6.7.

Prove
Theorem

6.6
by generalizing the strategy from
Lemmas

2.10
and
2.11
, which were used to prove
Theorem

2.12
.

6.8.

Hoare logic
is a formalism for reasoning about the correctness of programs
[
46
,
47
]
.
Its statements are
triples
denoted
\{P\}C\{Q\}
, where
P
and
Q
are logical formulas, and
C
is a program.
Such a triple
holds
when, if a machine starts in a state satisfying
P
, it reaches a state satisfying
Q
after executing
C
.

We can encode Hoare logic using
\mathsf{KAT}

[
48
]
, by writing

\{b\}e\{c\}\quad\quad\text{as a shorthand for}\quad\quad b\cdot e\cdot\overline{c}\equiv\texttt{0}

The idea is that, if
b\cdot e\cdot\overline{c}\equiv\texttt{0}
, then there is no valid way to execute
b\cdot e\cdot\overline{c}
, i.e., to assert that
b
holds, execute
e
, and then assert that
\overline{c}
holds.
Thus, necessarily, if
e
runs to completion after asserting
b
, then
c
holds.

In this exercise, you will verify that the rules of inference in Hoare logic are actually compatible with this encoding in
\mathsf{KAT}
.

(a)

Hoare logic contains a rule for sequential composition:

\frac{\{P\}C_{1}\{Q\}\quad\{Q\}C_{2}\{R\}\quad}{\{P\}C_{1};C_{2}\{R\}}

Verify the compatibility of this rule: given
b,c,d\in\mathsf{BExp}
and
e,f\in\textsf{KExp}
such that
\{b\}e\{c\}
and
\{c\}f\{d\}
hold, prove that
\{b\}e\cdot f\{d\}
holds.

(b)

The following rule governs
\mathbf{while}
-
\mathbf{do}
constructs.

\frac{\{P\wedge Q\}C\{P\}}{\{P\}\mathbf{while}\ Q\ \mathbf{do}\ C\{\neg Q\wedge P\}}

Verify this rule as well: given
b,c\in\mathsf{BExp}
and
e\in\textsf{KExp}
such that
\{b\cdot c\}e\{b\}
holds, prove that
\{b\}\mathbf{while}\ c\ \mathbf{do}\ e\{\overline{c}\cdot b\}
holds.

Hint: use the slide rule (
Lemma

2.24
).

(c)

We can alternatively encode a Hoare triple using either of the following equivalences:

b\cdot e\leqq e\cdot c\and b\cdot e\equiv b\cdot e\cdot c

Show that these three encodings are equivalent — i.e., if any of the three equivalences hold, then so do the others.

Chapter 7
Further Reading

In this booklet, we barely scratched the surface of the vast literature out there relating to
\mathsf{KA}
.
This chapter gives an (inevitably incomplete) list of pointers for further reading, organized along several themes.

Axiomatizing
\mathsf{KA}

Regular expressions were proposed by Kleene
[
3
]
, and the idea of encoding programs can be traced back to Pratt
[
4
]
.
There exists a variety of axioms for Kleene algebra; possibilities include the ones proposed by Salomaa
[
9
]
, Conway
[
7
]
, Krob
[
49
]
, Boffa
[
50
]
, and Kozen
[
6
]
.
The axioms presented in this booklet are due to Kozen
[
6
]
.
Each of these works also includes a completeness proof; as mentioned, the proof presented in this booklet is based on that of Jacobs
[
26
]
, who based his work on an earlier article by Kozen
[
8
]
.

We can also drop the right-handed fixpoint laws to obtain
left-handed
\mathsf{KA}

, which is still powerful enough to axiomatize equivalence of regular expressions
[
51
,
52
,
49
]
.
For an alternative proof of this we refer to
[
53
]
.
The latter work also offers some thoughts on a
finite model property
for Kleene algebra, which says that the equations that are true in all Kleene algebras are precisely those satisfied by
finite
Kleene algebras.
Palka first studied this property
[
54
]
, and related it to completeness.

Decision procedures

Equivalence in Kleene algebra is efficiently decidable, by converting regular expressions into automata, and checking bisimilarity.
There are various techniques to obtain automata from regular expressions, with equally various characteristics in terms of efficiency and complexity
[
17
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
18
]
; see
[
23
]
for an overview.

There are also different strategies for establishing whether two automata are bisimilar.
A rather efficient algorithm was proposed in
[
27
]
, where one can also find references to other procedures for checking bisimilarity.
A decision procedure for Kleene algebra that was proven correct in a proof assistant was provided in
[
55
]
.

Extensions

Kleene algebra captures reasoning about programs in a general setting.
However, for many applications, it is interesting to impose additional structure on the algebraic theory or add extra primitives to
\mathsf{KA}
.
Such extensions can be useful to express additional equivalences for programs that are not captured by the (rather general)
\mathsf{KA}
axioms, but are required in a specific domain.

The most prominent
\mathsf{KA}
extension is
\mathsf{KAT}

[
56
]
, which we reviewed in
Chapter

6
.

\mathsf{KAT}
was proven to be complete and decidable in
[
35
]
.
A more efficient decision procedure was developed and certified in
[
57
]
.

\mathsf{KAT}
subsumes
propositional Hoare logic
, in the sense that any valid rule of Hoare logic can be proved in KAT via the encoding from
6.8

[
48
]
.

Kleene algebra with observations
(
\mathsf{KAO}
)
[
58
]
is a variant of
\mathsf{KAT}
that does not identify concatenation of tests with conjunction — that is, the test
a\wedge b
is different from the test
a\cdot b
.
Instead, the axiom
a\wedge b\leq a\cdot b
is used, which semantically can be understood as “the behaviour of
a\wedge b
is included in the behaviour of
a\cdot b
”.
In a concurrent setting this is necessary, as
a\wedge b
is one event, but between
a
and
b
in
a\cdot b
, other events can take place in a parallel thread.
Hence,
a\wedge b
and
a\cdot b
do not necessarily have the same behaviour in a concurrent setting.

\mathsf{KAT}
has also been extended with mutable state in
[
59
]
and with networking primitives; the latter extension is known as
\mathsf{NetKAT}

[
34
]
.

\mathsf{NetKAT}
can be used to analyse packet behaviour in networks, and comes with an efficient (coalgebraic) decision procedure
[
60
]
.

Guarded Kleene Algebra with Tests
(
\mathsf{GKAT}
) was introduced
[
61
]
to formalise the fragment of
\mathsf{KAT}
that corresponds to programs formed using
\mathbf{if}
-
\mathbf{then}
-
\mathbf{else}
and
\mathbf{while}
-
\mathbf{do}
.
Deciding equivalence in
\mathsf{GKAT}
is easier than deciding equivalence in
\mathsf{KAT}
: whereas equivalence in
\mathsf{KAT}
is PSPACE-complete, in
\mathsf{GKAT}
it can be done in nearly linear time.
This result assumes that the number of tests is fixed; otherwise, the problem is co-NP hard
[
61
]
.
Right now, the completeness problem for
\mathsf{GKAT}
is not fully settled yet, though some progress has been made
[
62
,
63
,
64
]
.
An introduction to and survey of
\mathsf{GKAT}
can be found in
[
65
]
.

Kleene algebra with domain

[
66
]
extends
\mathsf{KA}
with axioms for a domain and codomain operation, which allows for the definition of modal operators.
Completeness of this extension has been settled, and several results about its complexity are also known
[
67
]

Modal Kleene algebra
is Kleene algebra enriched by forward and backward box and diamond operators, defined via domain and codomain operations
[
68
,
69
]
.
Both extensions can be thought of as a bridge between Kleene Algebra and Propositional Dynamic Logic (PDL)
[
70
,
71
]
.

Kleene algebra with a top element
, an extension relevant for reasoning about abnormal termination
[
72
]
, has been studied in
[
73
,
74
]
.
Completeness results are provided for language and relational models of
\mathsf{KA}
with top, and
\mathsf{KAT}
with top.
Kleene algebra with tests and top can be used to model
incorrectness logic

[
75
]
.
1
1

1

The completeness proof for
\mathsf{KAT}
with top given in this paper is incorrect, as discussed and fixed in
[
73
]
.

Hypotheses

Many of the aforementioned extensions of
\mathsf{KA}
come with proofs of completeness w.r.t. the appropriate language or relational models and decidability.
Such results are desirable for program verification — e.g., in a proof assistant, such structures make it possible to write algebraic proofs of correctness, and to mechanize some of the steps.
In particular, when two expressions
e
and
f
representing two programs happen to be provably equivalent in for instance
\mathsf{KAT}
, it suffices to call a certified decision procedure
[
55
,
57
]
.
Obtaining such decidability and completeness results is often non-trivial.

To develop a unifying perspective on extensions of
\mathsf{KA}
, and a more modular approach to proving completeness and decidability for new
\mathsf{KA}
extensions, a general framework called
Kleene algebra with hypotheses

[
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
76
]
was proposed, which encompasses for instance
\mathsf{KAT}
,
\mathsf{KAO}
and
\mathsf{NetKAT}

[
45
]
.
Kleene algebra with hypotheses comes with a canonical language model constructed from a set of hypotheses, in terms of
closed
languages
[
40
]
.
In the case of
\mathsf{KAT}
, the canonical language model obtained from hypotheses corresponds to the familiar interpretation of expressions as languages of guarded strings.
The hypotheses framework cannot be used when the resulting languages are not regular, which is the case for instance with commutative
\mathsf{KA}

[
77
,
78
,
7
]
.
We refer to
[
45
]
for an overview.

From a logical perspective, KA with hypotheses is about axiomatizing the
Horn theory
of regular expressions, which is about proving valid conditional equivalences between regular expressions, like the one from
2.8
.
The challenge is that such implications are undecidable in general
[
28
,
79
,
80
]
; the focus has therefore been in dealing with certain kinds of hypotheses, such as those of the form
e\equiv 0

[
39
,
81
]
.
Crucially, there are implications that are true in relational semantics, but not in all KAs, such as
e\leq 1\implies e^{2}=e
; for more on this, see
[
82
]
.

Bisimilarity

Kleene Algebra assumes left-distributivity, i.e., the program
e\cdot(f+g)
is equivalent to the program
e\cdot f+e\cdot g
— even though the latter program “chooses” whether or not to execute
f
or
g
before
e
is run.
Similarly, in
\mathsf{KA}

e\cdot 0
is equivalent to
0
— even though the former may perform some actions before ultimately failing.
To distinguish these, one can drop these laws from
\mathsf{KA}
by interpreting a regular expression up to bisimilarity instead.
In
[
83
]
a sound axiomatisation of bisimilarity for regular expressions is provided.
A completeness proof for a fragment of this syntax can be found in
[
84
]
; a proof of completeness for the entire syntax has also been announced
[
85
]
.
Part of what makes this version of
\mathsf{KA}
so hard is that not every automaton can be translated back to a bisimilar expression
[
83
]
, and thus one half of Kleene’s theorem, which was pivotal in the completeness proof, is missing.

There is limited work on bisimulation semantics for variants of
\mathsf{KA}
.
Simulation rather than bisimulation is characterised in probabilistic versions of Kleene algebra
[
86
,
87
]
.

Probabilistic
\mathsf{KA}

was introduced for resolving non-deterministic choices as they occur.
Completeness of probabilistic
\mathsf{KA}
w.r.t. an appropriate class of automata is considered in
[
88
]
.

(Probabilistic)
\mathsf{GKAT}
modulo bisimilarity
is studied in
[
89
,
62
]
.

Concurrency

The aforementioned extensions of Kleene algebra all consider programs in a sequential setting.
Kleene algebras that model programs with parallel behaviour have also been studied widely.

Concurrent Kleene algebra
was proposed in
[
90
,
91
]
, and models concurrency via an axiom called the exchange law as a combination of interleaving and certain parts of programs taking place “truly concurrently” (i.e., there is no causal relation between these events).
An overview of the history of
\mathsf{CKA}
and the exchange law can be found in
[
92
]
.
Completeness of
\mathsf{CKA}
is established in
[
93
,
94
]
, both of which are based on
[
95
]
.

Adding tests to
\mathsf{CKA}
is hard — indeed, curious inconsistencies may crop up
[
58
]
.

\mathsf{KAO}
circumvents these, which allows an extension to
concurrent Kleene algebra with observations
(
\mathsf{CKAO}
), which in turn gives rise to further extensions to analyse concurrent programs with state (
\mathsf{POCKA}
)
[
96
]
and concurrent behaviours in networks (
\mathsf{CNetKAT}
)
[
97
]
; these are all developed using
\mathsf{KA}
with hypotheses.

\mathsf{DyNetKAT}

[
98
]
is a different concurrent extension of
\mathsf{NetKAT}
that does not use
\mathsf{CKA}
but instead uses a parallel operator in the style of process algebra.

Synchronous Kleene algebra
(
\mathsf{SKA}
)
[
99
]
was proposed as a way to model lock-step concurrency using
\mathsf{KA}
.
The appropriate language model for
\mathsf{SKA}
is much simpler than for
\mathsf{CKA}
, but less expressive because arbitrary interleaving between programs is not possible.
In fact,
\mathsf{SKA}
is an instance of Kleene algebra with hypotheses, and not of concurrent Kleene algebra with hypotheses
[
76
]
.
Completeness is established in
[
100
]
.

Probabilistic
\mathsf{CKA}

[
101
,
102
]
can capture non-determinism, concurrency and probability using a “truly concurrent” model.
Its algebraic theory is shown to be sound for a set of probabilistic automata modulo probabilistic simulation; completeness appears to be an open question.

Finally, there is work on
higher-dimensional automata
(HDAs)
[
103
]
, which model the beginning and end of concurrent events using higher-dimensional structure.
This allows them to describe concurrent behavior like message passing.
While equational reasoning has been less of a concern in this line of work, HDAs do enjoy many of the formal results that one expects from plain automata
[
104
,
105
]
, so it is conceivable that the results around
\mathsf{KA}
can one day be extended to this setting.
